is Harrop, then
\mathbf{H}(A)\leftrightarrow\,\mathbf{r}\,A
.

2.

If
E
is an nc expression, then
\mathbf{H}(E)=E
, in particular
\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{False})=\mathbf{False}
.

{exa}

[
Realiser of induction and co-induction
]
Set

\displaystyle f\circ g\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda a\,.\,f(g\,a),

\displaystyle\hbox{$[f+g]$}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda c\,.\,\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to f\,a;\mathbf{Right}(b)\to g\,b\}.

Note that if
f:\rho\to\sigma
and
g:\sigma\to\sigma^{\prime}
, then

g\circ f:\rho\to\sigma^{\prime}
,
and if
f_{1}:\rho_{1}\to\sigma
and
f_{2}:\rho_{2}\to\sigma
, then

[f_{1}+f_{2}]:(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})\to\sigma
.

Note also that for a s.p. non-Harrop operator
\Phi
,
and a non-Harrop predicate
P
.

\tau(\mu(\Phi))=\tau(\nu(\Phi))=\mathbf{fix}\,\tau(\Phi)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{fix}\,\,\alpha\,.\,\tau(\Phi)(\alpha)\,

For every s.p. type operator
\varphi
let

\mathbf{mon}_{\varphi}:(\alpha\to\beta)\to\varphi(\alpha)\to\varphi(\beta)

be the canonical program such that
for every s.p. operator
\Phi
and all predicates
P,Q
(of fitting arity),

\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi)}
realizes
(P\subseteq Q)\to\Phi(P)\subseteq\Phi(Q)
.
Then
\mathbf{mon}_{\varphi}
is a polymorphic program whose type depends on the type variables

\alpha,\beta
. These type variables may be substituted by any types
\rho,\sigma
.
We sometimes write
\mathbf{mon}^{\rho,\sigma}_{\varphi}
to indicate that we are interested
in the typing obtained by this substitution, that is,

\mathbf{mon}^{\rho,\sigma}_{\varphi}:(\rho\to\sigma)\to\varphi(\rho)\to\varphi(\sigma)
.
A similar convention applies to the polymorphic programs defined below, such as

\mathbf{it}_{\varphi}
,
\mathbf{coit}_{\varphi}
, etc., as well as to the polymorphic constructors

\mathbf{Left}^{\alpha,\beta}:\alpha\to(\alpha+\beta)
,

\mathbf{Right}^{\alpha,\beta}:\beta\to(\alpha+\beta)
, and the identity function

\mathbf{id}^{\alpha}:\alpha\to\alpha
.
Of course these programs do not depend on the superscripts but only
on the subscript (if any).

To improve readability we will in the following omit the ‘
\tau
’ from
\tau(A)
,

\tau(P)
and
\tau(\Phi)
and we write
\nu\Phi
instead of

\nu(\Phi)
, etc. Hence for example, instead of writing

\mathbf{mon}^{\tau(P),\tau(\nu(\Phi))}_{\tau(\Phi)}:(\tau(P)\to\tau(\nu(\Phi)))\to\tau(\Phi)(\tau(P))\to\tau(\Phi)(\tau(\nu(\Phi)))

we write

\mathbf{mon}^{P,\nu\Phi}_{\Phi}:(P\to\nu\Phi)\to\Phi(P)\to\Phi(\nu\Phi)

or even

\mathbf{mon}^{P,\nu\Phi}_{\Phi}:(P\to\nu\Phi)\to\Phi(P)\to\nu\Phi

since
\tau(\Phi)(\tau(\nu\Phi))\equiv\tau(\nu\Phi)
.

Induction.

If
s:\Phi(P)\to P
realises
\Phi(P)\subseteq P
, then

\mathbf{it}^{P}_{\Phi}\,s
realizes
\mu\Phi\subseteq P
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{it}_{\varphi}:(\varphi(\alpha)\to\alpha)\to\mathbf{fix}\,(\varphi)\to\alpha,

\displaystyle\mathbf{it}_{\varphi}\,s\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{rec}\,\,\lambda f.\,s\circ\mathbf{mon}^{\mathbf{fix}\,(\varphi),\alpha}_{\varphi}\,f.

Co-induction.

If
s:P\to\Phi(P)
realises
P\subseteq\Phi(P)
, then

\mathbf{coit}^{P}_{\Phi}\,s
realises
P\subseteq\nu\Phi
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{coit}_{\varphi}:(\alpha\to\varphi(\alpha))\to\alpha\to\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi,

\displaystyle\mathbf{coit}_{\varphi}\,s\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{rec}\,\,\lambda f.\,\mathbf{mon}^{\alpha,\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi}_{\varphi}\,f\circ s.

Half-strong co-induction.

If
s:P\to(\Phi(P)+\nu\Phi)

realises
P\subseteq\Phi(P)\cup\nu\Phi
, then

\mathbf{hscoit}^{P}_{\Phi}\,s

realises
P\subseteq\nu\Phi
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{hscoit}_{\varphi}:(\alpha\to(\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi))\to\alpha\to\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi

\displaystyle\mathbf{hscoit}_{\varphi}\,s\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{rec}\,\,\lambda f.\,[\mathbf{mon}^{\alpha,\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi}_{\varphi}\,f+\mathbf{id}^{\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi}]\circ s

Strong co-induction.

If
s:P\to(\Phi(P)+\nu\Phi)

realises
P\subseteq\Phi(P\cup\nu(\Phi))
, then

\mathbf{scoit}^{P}_{\Phi}\,s

realises
P\subseteq\nu\Phi
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{scoit}_{\varphi}:(\alpha\to\varphi(\alpha+\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi))\to\alpha\to\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi,

\displaystyle\mathbf{scoit}_{\varphi}\,s\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{rec}\,\,\lambda f.\,\mathbf{mon}^{\alpha+\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi,\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi}_{\varphi}\,[f+\mathbf{id}^{\mathbf{fix}\,\varphi}]\circ s.

Generalised half-strong co-induction.

Assume
\Phi^{\prime}
is monotone.

Let
\mathbf{absorb}^{\alpha}_{\Phi^{\prime},\Phi}:\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi(\alpha))\to\Phi(\alpha)

realise
\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi(Y))\subseteq\Phi(Y)
for all
Y
.

If
s:P\to\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi(P)+\nu\Phi)

realises
P\subseteq\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi(P)\cup\nu(\Phi))
, then

\mathbf{hscoit}_{\Phi}\,(\mathbf{absorb}_{\Phi^{\prime},\Phi}\circ\mathbf{mon}_{\Phi^{\prime}}[\mathbf{mon}_{\Phi}\,\mathbf{Left}+\mathbf{mon}_{\Phi}\,\mathbf{Right}]\circ s)

realises
P\subseteq\nu\Phi
. This means that the realiser is a function

f:P\to\nu\Phi
defined recursively by

\displaystyle f\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}^{P+\nu\Phi,\nu\Phi}_{\Phi}\,[f+\mathbf{id}^{\nu\Phi}]

\displaystyle\circ\ \mathbf{absorb}^{P+\nu\Phi}_{\Phi^{\prime},\Phi}

\displaystyle\circ\ \mathbf{mon}^{\Phi(P)+\nu\Phi,\Phi(P+\nu\Phi)}_{\Phi^{\prime}}[\mathbf{mon}^{P,P+\nu\Phi}_{\Phi}\,\mathbf{Left}^{P,\nu\Phi}+\mathbf{mon}^{\nu\Phi,P+\nu\Phi}_{\Phi}\,\mathbf{Right}^{P,\nu\Phi}]

\displaystyle\circ\ s.

{exa}

[
Realiser of well-founded induction
]
The schema of well-founded induction,
\textbf{WFI}_{\prec,A}
(P), is realised as follows: If
s
realises
\textbf{Prog}_{\prec,A}
where
P
is non-Harrop, then
\mathbf{Acc}_{\prec}\cap A\subseteq P
is realised by

•

\tilde{f}\overset{\text{rec}}{=}\lambda a.\,(s\,a\,(\lambda a^{\prime}.\,\lambda b.\,\tilde{f}\,a^{\prime}))
if
\prec
and
A
are both non-Harrop,

•

\tilde{f}\overset{\text{rec}}{=}\lambda a.\,(s\,a\,\tilde{f})
if
\prec
is Harrop and
A
is non-Harrop,

•

\tilde{c}\overset{\text{rec}}{=}s\,(\lambda b.\,\tilde{c})
if
\prec
is non-Harrop and
A
is Harrop,

•

\mathbf{rec}\,s
if
\prec
and
A
are both Harrop.

See
[
BT21a
, Lemma 21]
for a proof.

4.5.
Soundness

The Soundness Theorem
[
BT21a
]
stating that provable formulas are realisable is the theoretical foundation for program extraction.

Theorem 10

(
Soundness
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a set of nc axioms. From an
IFP(
\mathcal{A}
)
proof of formula
A
one can extract a program
M:\tau(A)
such that
M\,\mathbf{r}\,A
is provable in
RIFP(
\mathcal{A}
)
.

More generally, let
\Gamma
be a set of Harrop formulas and
\Delta
a set of non-Harrop formulas. Then, from an
IFP(
\mathcal{A}
)
proof of a formula
A
from the assumptions
\Gamma,\Delta
one can extract a program
M
with
\emph{FV($M$)}\subseteq\vec{u}
such that
\vec{u}:\tau(\Delta)\vdash M:\tau(A)
and
M\,\mathbf{r}\,A
are provable in
RIFP(
\mathcal{A}
)
from the assumptions
\mathbf{H}(\Gamma)
and
\vec{u}\,\mathbf{r}\,\Delta
.

If one wants to apply this theorem to obtain a program realising formula
A
one must provide terms
K_{1},\ldots,K_{n}
realising the assumptions in
\Delta
. Then it follows that the term
M(K_{1},\ldots,K_{n})
realises
A
, provably in RIFP. Because the program axioms of RIFP given in
[
BT21a
]
are correct with respect to the denotational semantics, a further consequence is that
M(K_{1},\ldots,K_{n})
is a correct realiser of
A
.

That realisers do actually
compute
witnesses is shown in
[
BT21a
]
by two
Computational Adequacy Theorems
that relate the denotational definition of realisability with a lazy operational semantics.

5.
Computationally motivated logical connectives

Non-termination is a natural and fundamental phenomenon in computation.
It is denotationally modelled in domain theory
[
GHKLMS03
]
and a logical account
of it is Scott’s logic with an existence predicate
[
Sc79
]
.
The Minlog system
[
Min11
]

supports the extraction of programs that may or may not terminate and
keeps control of potential partiality through a logic with totality degrees.
A limitation of programs extracted from proofs in Minlog, or other systems
such as Coq
[
Let02
]
, is that they are sequential. Having the
possibility of running computations concurrently, on the other hand,
can be very useful to get around partiality. If, e.g.,
M
and
N
are
two programs known to realise formula
A
under the assumption that
condition
B
or
\neg B
holds, respectively, then at least one of
them is guaranteed to terminate. So running them concurrently and
picking the result obtained first, will lead to a result realising

A
, provided that if
M
or
N
terminates then it realises
A
.

To capture realisability restricted to a condition as described above,
we follow the approach in
[
BT21b
]
and extend in Section
5.1
IFP
by a propositional connective

A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\qquad\hbox{(``$A$ \emph{restricted} to $B$'')}

which, for nc-formulas
B
, has a similar meaning as the formula
B\to A
but
behaves slightly differently (and better) with respect to realisability: While a realiser of

B\to A
is a program that realises
A
if
B
holds
but otherwise provides no guarantees,
a realiser of
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
is a program
p
that terminates and realises
A
if
B
holds,
but even if
B
does not hold,
p
will realise
A
provided
p
terminates.
In order to behave well, the formation of
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
is restricted to formulas
A

satisfying a syntactic condition called productivity (defined in Section
5.1
)
that guarantees that only terminating programs can realise
A
.

In Section
5.2
we introduce the concurrency modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)

from
[
BT21b
]
with the crucial rule

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\neg B}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-lem)}

that makes precise the above intuition. We also prove the realisability of a couple
of further rules that say how
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
interacts with other logical connectives.

Finally, in Section
5.3
,
we introduce a new concurrency modality,

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
, which inherits most of the properties of
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)

but in addition has realisable rules corresponding to a monad.
This new modality will be used
in Section
7
to define concurrent versions of the signed digit
representation and infinite Gray code which are constructively equivalent.

5.1.
Restriction
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}

Following
[
BT21b
]
, we introduce restriction,
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
, where
A
is required
to be
productive
4
4

4

Observe that in
[
BT21b
]
the notion “strict” is used instead of “productive”.
, that is,
every implication and restriction in
A
has to be part of a Harrop formula
or a disjunction. In particular, Harrop formulas and disjunctions are
always productive.
The reason why
A
is required to be productive is that this ensures that
A

has only defined realisers, that is
\bot
does not realise
A
.

The definition of the Harrop property is extended by demanding
that Harrop formulas must not contain a restriction at a strictly
positive position. In particular, restrictions are not
Harrop. Realisability for restrictions is defined as

a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}a:\tau(A)\wedge(\,\mathbf{r}\,B\to a\neq\bot)\wedge(a\neq\bot\to a\,\mathbf{r}\,A).

Note that if
A
is a Harrop formula, then
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
is equivalent to the formula

(\,\mathbf{r}\,B\vee a\not=\bot)\to(a=\mathbf{Nil}\wedge\mathbf{H}(A)).

The type of restriction is
\tau(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\tau(A)
.

To gain some intuition suppose that a closed program
M
realises

A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
. Since closed programs denote a value different from
\bot

exactly if they reduce to whnf, one has:
(i) If
B
is realisable, then
M
reduces to whnf.
(ii) If
M
reduces to whnf, then
M
realises
A
(even if
B
is not
realisable). In this sense, one has partial correctness of
M
with
respect to the specification
A
. The distinction between

A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
and
B\to A
regarding realisability is carefully
discussed in
[
BT21b
]
.

Sometimes, we need to get rid of the productivity requirement. This can be achieved by considering formulas of kind
A\lor\mathbf{False}
instead of just
A
. By definition,
A\lor\mathbf{False}
is always productive. Moreover,
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A
, exactly if
\mathbf{Left}(a)\,\mathbf{r}\,A\lor\mathbf{False}
. Note here that
\mathbf{False}
has no realiser. This leads us to the following unrestricted version of the restriction connective

A\mathord{\upharpoonright}^{\rm u}_{B}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(A\lor\mathbf{False})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}.

Since the realisers of such formulas are more complicated than those in the productive case, we keep both versions of the restriction connective. It should be clear from the definition that all statements in this paper about the realisability of rules for the restriction connective
\mathord{\restriction}
also hold for the unrestricted version
\mathord{\upharpoonright}^{\rm u}
.

The following derivation rules concerning restriction are added to IFP:

\dfrac{B\to A_{0}\vee A_{1}\quad\neg B\to A_{0}\wedge A_{1}}{(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\restriction}$-intro ($A_{0},A_{1},B$ Harrop))}

\dfrac{A}{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-return)

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad A\to(A^{\prime}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})}{A^{\prime}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-bind)

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad B^{\prime}\to B}{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B^{\prime}}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-antimon)

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad B}{A}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-mp)

\dfrac{}{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{False}}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-efq)

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\neg\neg B}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-stab)

\dfrac{(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\mathord{\upharpoonright}^{\rm u}_{C}}{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B\land C}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-absorb)

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{(A\land C)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}

(
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\land
)
.

{lemC}

[
[
BT21b
]
]
The rules for restriction are realisable, provably in extended
RIFP
. Hence, Soundness Theorem
10
remains valid for the extension of
IFP
by restriction, however, classical logic is needed to derive the correctness of realisers.

Note that the last two rules have not been considered in
[
BT21b
]
. As is easily verified, Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-absorb
) is realised by
\lambda a.\,\mathbf{case}\,a\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a^{\prime})\to a^{\prime}\}

and Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\land
) by
(\mathbf{Pair}\mathopen{\downarrow})\mathopen{\downarrow}
, where
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a
denotes
strict application
:

f\mathopen{\downarrow}a\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,a\,\mathbf{of}\,\{C(\_)\to f\,a\mid C\in\{\mathbf{Nil},\mathbf{Left},\mathbf{Right},\mathbf{Pair},\mathbf{fun}\}\}.

Observe that
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a=f\,a
if
a\not=\bot
and
f\mathopen{\downarrow}\bot=\bot
.

{lemC}

[
[
BT21b
]
]
The following rule is derivable from the rules for restriction:

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad A\to A^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}\,\,\emph{(\mathord{\restriction}-mon)}.

The rule is realised by
\lambda f.\,\lambda a.\,f\mathopen{\downarrow}a
. To see this assume that
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
and
f\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\to A^{\prime})
. We have to show that
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a\,\mathbf{r}\,A^{\prime}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
. Suppose first that
\,\mathbf{r}\,B
. Then
a\neq\bot
and hence by definition of
f\mathopen{\downarrow}
,
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a=f\,a
. We need that
f\,a\neq\bot
. Here, the productivity requirement for the restriction connective comes into play:
A^{\prime}
needs to be productive and since
f\,a\,\mathbf{r}\,A^{\prime}
, we have that
f\,a\neq\bot
, as was to be shown. Next, suppose that
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a\neq\bot
. Then
a\neq\bot
as well, by definition of
f\mathopen{\downarrow}
. Therefore,
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A
. It follows that
f\,a\,\mathbf{r}\,A^{\prime}
. Since
a\neq\bot
, we moreover have that
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a=f\,a
. Thus,
f\mathopen{\downarrow}a\,\mathbf{r}\,A^{\prime}
.

5.2.
McCarthy’s Amb and the concurrency modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)

To deal with concurrency,
[
BT21b
]
introduced a
further constructor
\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
indicating that its arguments
a,b

need be evaluated concurrently in order to obtain one of the results
even if the other one is not terminating. The domain
D
now has to
satisfy the domain equation

D=(\mathbf{Nil}+\mathbf{Left}(D)+\mathbf{Right}(D)+\mathbf{Pair}(D\times D)+\mathbf{Fun}(D\to D)+\mathbf{Amb}(D\times D))_{\bot}.

The programming language is extended by a constructor
\mathbf{Amb}
which denotes the constructor
\mathbf{Amb}

in the domain
D
.
Hence, denotationally, the constructor
\mathbf{Amb}
is an exact copy of
\mathbf{Pair}
, that is,
it acts like a lazy pairing operator.
Only the operational semantics interprets a program
\mathbf{Amb}(M,N)
as a concurrent
computation of
M
and
N
until one of them is reduced to whnf.
This is formalised by the following (non-deterministic) relation
\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}
(‘c’ for ‘choice’):

ci.

M\rightsquigarrow M^{\prime}

M\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}M^{\prime}

,

cii.

\mathbf{Amb}(M_{1},M_{2})\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}M_{i}
if
M_{i}
is a whnf (
i=1,2
),

ciii.

M_{i}\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}M_{i}^{\prime}

(i=1,\ldots,k)

C(M_{1},\ldots,M_{k})\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}C(M_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,,M_{k}^{\prime})

if
C\neq\mathbf{Amb}
.

The deterministic relation
\rightsquigarrow
which
\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}
extends is now to be understood with respect
to all constructors, including
\mathbf{Amb}
.
The intuition of
\overset{\mathrm{c}}{\rightsquigarrow}
is that a program is first deterministically evaluated using
\rightsquigarrow
.
If a program of the form
\mathbf{Amb}(M_{1},M_{2})
is obtained, deterministic computation continuous in
parallel with
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.
As soon as one of the two programs reach a whnf, the other may be discarded using Rule (cii).
Rule (ciii) says that the computation can be carried out inside (nested)
data constructors.
Note that rule (cii) cannot be applied to proper subterms of a term
\mathbf{Amb}(M_{1},M_{2})

since the only way of reducing
\mathbf{Amb}(M_{1},M_{2})
with a rule other than (cii) is by Rule (ci) and
Rule (vi) of
\rightsquigarrow
.
This ensures that at any point only two concurrent threads are needed to carry out the computation.
For the reductions to yield the desired result (see
[
BT21b
]
, Theorems 1 and 2), fairness conditions
must be imposed. For example, if in
\mathbf{Amb}(M_{1},M_{2})
at least one of the
M_{i}
is hnf, then
Rule (cii) will eventually be applied.
In
[
BT21b
]
slighty more general (and more complicated) but essentially equivalent rules are
given which facilitate the formalisation of the fairness condition and allow parallel threads
to be evaluated at differend ‘speeds’.

To indicate at the logical level that a realiser of a formula
A
may be computed concurrently,
a new modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
was introduced in
[
BT21b
]
where, again, the formula

A
is required to be productive.
For a non-Harrop formula
A
realisability is defined as

\displaystyle c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\,

\displaystyle c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\wedge a,b:\tau(A)\wedge\mbox{}

\displaystyle(a\neq\bot\vee b\neq\bot)\wedge\mbox{}

\displaystyle(a\neq\bot\to a\,\mathbf{r}\,A)\wedge(b\neq\bot\to b\,\mathbf{r}\,A).

Thus, a realiser of
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
is a pair of candidate realisers
a

and
b
at least one of which denotes a defined value
and all of
a
and
b
which are defined values are
correct realisers. In particular, if
a
and
b
are both defined,
then they are
both
correct realisers. Therefore, by running the
two programs for the candidates
a
and
b
concurrently and taking
the one which becomes defined (i.e. a whnf) first, it is guaranteed that
we obtain a correct result. Hence, we can safely stop the
other process.

The occurrence of
A
in
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
is regarded strictly
positive. The definition of the Harrop property is further extended by demanding
that Harrop formulas must not contain the concurrency operator at a strictly
positive position. In particular,
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
is always non-Harrop
(even if
A
is Harrop).
The type of the concurrency modality is
\tau(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A))=\mathbf{A}(\tau(A))

where
\mathbf{A}
is a new type operator.

IFP is once more extended by adding the following derivation rules. The logical system thus obtained is called
Concurrent Fixed Point Logic (CFP)
.

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C}\quad A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\neg C}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-lem)}

\dfrac{A}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-return)}

\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\quad A\to B}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-mon)}

\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}{A}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-H ($A$ Harrop))}

\rule{0.0pt}{22.76219pt}\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-absorb)}

\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{D}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor C)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B\lor D}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-$\lor$)}

\rule{0.0pt}{22.76219pt}\dfrac{B\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\quad\neg B\to A_{0}\wedge A_{1}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-intro ($A_{0},A_{1},B$ Harrop))}

Note that the last three rules have not been considered in
[
BT21b
]
.

Lemma 11
.

The rules for the concurrency modality are realisable.

Proof 5.1
.

The realisability of the first four rules has been shown in
[
BT21b
]
. It remains to consider the last three rules.

It is easy to see that (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-absorb
) is realised by the identity function:
Assume
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})
.
Then
c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
. Furthermore,
a\neq\bot
or
b\neq\bot
, and
in the first case
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
while in the second case
b\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
.
We show that
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
.
By the facts we know about
c
, it suffices to show that if
a\neq\bot
then
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A

(and similarly for
b
). But if
a\neq\bot
, then
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
and hence
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A
.

Next, we show that Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
) is realised by

g\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda a.\,\lambda b.\,\mathbf{Amb}_{\mathbf{LR}}(\mathbf{Left}\mathopen{\downarrow}a,\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}b).

where
\mathbf{Amb}_{\mathbf{LR}}(u,v)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
if
u=\mathbf{Left}(\_)
or
v=\mathbf{Right}(\_)
and
\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\bot
otherwise.

\mathbf{Amb}_{\mathbf{LR}}
can be easily defined using the case construct.

Suppose that
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
and
b\,\mathbf{r}\,C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{D}
.
We have to verify that

1.

\,\mathbf{r}\,(B\lor D)\to g\,a\,b\neq\bot
.

2.

g\,a\,b\neq\bot\to(g\,a\,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor C)
.

(
1
) Assume
B\lor D
is realisable. Then
B
or
D
is realisable.
Without restriction assume
\,\mathbf{r}\,B
. Then
a\neq\bot
and hence

\mathbf{Left}\mathopen{\downarrow}a=\mathbf{Left}(a)
and
g\,a\,b=\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}(a),\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}b)\neq\bot
.

(
2
) If
g\,a\,b\neq\bot
, then

g\,a\,b=\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
where
u=\mathbf{Left}\mathopen{\downarrow}a
and
v=\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}b
,
and
u\neq\bot
or
v\neq\bot
.
Furthermore, if
u\neq\bot
, then
u=\mathbf{Left}(a)
where
a\neq\bot
.
Hence
a\,\mathbf{r}\,A
and therefore
u\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor C)
.
With a similar argument one sees that if
v\neq\bot
, then

v\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor C)
.

Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro
), finally, is realised by

h\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda c.\,\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Amb}(C(\_),\_)\to c;\mathbf{Amb}(\_,C(\_))\to c\mid C\in\{\mathbf{Left},\mathbf{Right}\}\}.

Observe the overlapping clauses. First we note that for

c:\mathbf{A}(\tau(A_{0}\lor A_{1}))
:

(*):

If
c
is of the form
\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
, where

a\neq\bot
or
b\neq\bot
, then
h\,c=c
. This is the case, in particular, if
c
realises

\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
.

(**):

If
h\,c\neq\bot
, then
h\,c=c
and
c
is of the form
\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)

where
a\neq\bot
or
b\neq\bot
.

Now, assume
c
realises

B\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
, that is,
c:\mathbf{A}(\tau(A_{0}\lor A_{1}))
and

\mathbf{H}(B)\to c\,\mathbf{r}\,(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1}))
,
and that
\mathbf{H}(\neg B\to A_{0}\wedge A_{1})
holds, that is,

\neg\mathbf{H}(B)\to\mathbf{H}(A_{0})\wedge\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
.
We show that
h\,c
realises
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
:

First, assume
\mathbf{H}(B)
. Then
c
realises
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
.
Hence, by (*),
h\,c=c\neq\bot
.

Next, suppose
h\,c\neq\bot
. Then, by (**),
h\,c=c
and

c
is of the form
\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
where
a\neq\bot
or
b\neq\bot
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that
c
realises
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
.
We do a classical case analysis on
\mathbf{H}(B)
.
If
\mathbf{H}(B)
holds, then
c
realises
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
.
If
\mathbf{H}(B)
does not hold, then
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
hold.
To prove that
c
realises
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})
it suffices to show
that whenever
a
or
b
are defined, then they realise
A_{0}\lor A_{1}
.
Since
c:\mathbf{A}(\tau(A_{0}\lor A_{1}))
, we have
a,b\in\tau(A_{0}\lor A_{1})
.
But, since
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
hold, every defined element
in
\tau(A_{0}\lor A_{1})
realises
A_{0}\lor A_{1}
.

We summarise the realisers obtained by displaying the rules above with their realisers.
We restrict Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-mon) to the most interesting cases where
A
and
B
are
both non-Harrop. In this case we need for the rule the program

\mathbf{mapamb}\,\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda f.\,\lambda c.\,\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\to\mathbf{Amb}(f\mathopen{\downarrow}a,f\mathopen{\downarrow}b)\}.

\dfrac{a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C}\quad b\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\neg C}}{\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-lem)}

\dfrac{a\,\mathbf{r}\,A}{\mathbf{Amb}(a,\bot)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-return)}

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\quad f\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\to B)}{(\mathbf{mapamb}\,f\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-mon, $A,B$ non-Harrop)}

\dfrac{\mathbf{H}(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A))}{\mathbf{H}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-H ($A$ Harrop))}

\dfrac{a\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\quad b\,\mathbf{r}\,C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{D}}{(g\,a\,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor C)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B\lor D}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-$\lor$)}

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})}{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-absorb)}

\rule{0.0pt}{22.76219pt}\dfrac{\mathbf{H}(B)\to c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\quad\neg\mathbf{H}(B)\to\mathbf{H}(A_{0})\wedge\mathbf{H}(A_{1})}{(h\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-intro ($A_{0},A_{1},B$ Harrop))}

where
g,h
are as defined in the proof.

Lemma 12
.

The following rules are derivable in
CFP
:

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\dfrac{\neg\neg(A\vee B)\quad C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\quad C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(C)}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-elim)}&\dfrac{\neg\neg(A\vee B)\quad C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\quad D\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(C\vee D)}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-elim-or)}\\[12.91663pt]
\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee B)}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\vee\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B))}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-dist)}&\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\wedge B)}{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\wedge\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B)}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\wedge$-dist).}\end{array}

Proof 5.2
.

Rules (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\vee
-elim
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\vee
-elim-or
) have already been considered in
[
BT21b
]
.
The Rules (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\vee
-dist
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\wedge
-dist
) are
easy consequences of the Rules (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-return
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-mon
).

Let us again display the rules with their realisers.

\dfrac{\neg\mathbf{H}(\neg(A\vee B))\quad a\,\mathbf{r}\,C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\quad b\,\mathbf{r}\,C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(C)}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-elim)}

\dfrac{\neg\mathbf{H}(\neg(A\vee B))\quad a\,\mathbf{r}\,C\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{A}\quad b\,\mathbf{r}\,D\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}{\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}\mathopen{\downarrow}a,\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(C\vee D)}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-elim-or)}

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee B)}{(\mathbf{mapamb}\,g\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\vee\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B))}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\vee$-dist)}

where
g\,d\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Amb}(a,\bot));\mathbf{Right}(b)\to\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Amb}(b,\bot))\}

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\wedge B)}{\mathbf{Pair}(\mathbf{mapamb}\,g_{1}\,c,\mathbf{mapamb}\,g_{2}\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\wedge\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B))}\,\,\emph{($\mathord{\downdownarrows}$-$\wedge$-dist)}

where
g_{i}\,d\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Pair}(a_{1},a_{2})\to\mathbf{Amb}(a_{i},\bot)\}
.

5.3.
The monadic concurrency modality
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)

It is easy to see that the concurrency modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
is not a monad.
The monadic lifting law
(A\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B))\to(\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(B))

is in general not realisable.
In order to turn it into a monad we use its finite iterative closure

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\overset{\mu}{=}\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)).

Note that
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
is defined for arbitrary formulas
A
(not only productive ones)
since in its definition
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
is applied to a disjunction.
As follows from the definition, we have for
c:\delta
,

\displaystyle c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\overset{\mu}{=}\,\,

\displaystyle c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\wedge a,b:\tau(A\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))\wedge(a\neq\bot\lor b\neq\bot)\land\mbox{}

\displaystyle(a\neq\bot\to(a=\mathbf{Left}(a^{\prime})\wedge a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,A)\vee(a=\mathbf{Right}(a^{\prime\prime})\wedge a^{\prime\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)))\land\mbox{}

\displaystyle(b\neq\bot\to(b=\mathbf{Left}(b^{\prime})\wedge b^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,A)\vee(b=\mathbf{Right}(b^{\prime\prime})\wedge b^{\prime\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))).

As we will see next, in case of the iterated concurrency modality
the following analogue of Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
–
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro
) modality is realisable.
Again
A_{0},A_{1},B
are required to be Harrop:

\dfrac{B\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\quad\neg B\to A_{0}\wedge A_{1}}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A_{0}\vee A_{1})\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}$-$\mathord{\restriction}$-intro)}.

We need the following functions
\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}},\triangleright:D\times D\to D
and
f^{*},h^{+},g\colon D\to D
:

\displaystyle a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}b\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,\mathbf{Pair}(a,b)\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Pair}(\mathbf{Nil},\_)\to\mathbf{Nil};\mathbf{Pair}(\_,\mathbf{Nil})\to\mathbf{Nil}\},

\displaystyle d\triangleright a\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Nil}\to a\},

\displaystyle f^{*}d\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil}))\to\mathbf{Nil};

\displaystyle\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil}))\to\mathbf{Nil};

\displaystyle\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Amb}(u,v))\to f^{*}u\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}v\},

\displaystyle h^{+}c\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\to

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{case}\,f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Nil}\to

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\mathbf{Amb}(f^{*}a\triangleright g\,a,f^{*}b\triangleright g\,b)\}

\displaystyle\},

g\,d\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(\_)\to d;\mathbf{Right}(e)\to\mathbf{Right}(h^{+}e)\}.

Lemma 13
.

Assume
A=A_{0}\lor A_{1}
where
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are Harrop formulas.

1.

(\forall a,b)\,(\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\to f^{*}a=\mathbf{Nil}\lor f^{*}b=\mathbf{Nil})
.

2.

(\forall c)\,(c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\to(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.

3.

If
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
,
then
(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A_{0}\lor A_{1})
, for all
c
such that
h^{+}c

is of the form
\mathbf{Amb}(\_,\_)
.

Proof 5.3
.

(
1
)
We use s.p. induction.
If
\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
, then
a\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
,
or
b\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.
Without restriction assume the former.
If
a=\mathbf{Left}(d)
where
d\,\mathbf{r}\,A
, then
f^{*}a=\mathbf{Nil}
.
If
a=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Amb}(u,v))
where
\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
, then,
by the induction hypothesis,
f^{*}u=\mathbf{Nil}
or
f^{*}v=\mathbf{Nil}
.
Hence
f^{*}a=\mathbf{Nil}
.

(
2
) Again, we use s.p. induction.
Assume
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
Then, by (
1
),
c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
with

f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b=\mathbf{Nil}
and

h^{+}c=\mathbf{Amb}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime}=f^{*}a\triangleright g\,a
and

b^{\prime}=f^{*}b\triangleright g\,b
.
Since
f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b=\mathbf{Nil}
,

a^{\prime}=g\,a\neq\bot
or
b^{\prime}=g\,b\neq\bot
, as required.
It remains to show that every defined element of
\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}
realises

A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
Without restriction let
a^{\prime}
be defined. Hence
a^{\prime}=g\,a

and either (i)
g\,a=a=\mathbf{Left}(p)
and thus
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Left}(p)

with
p\in\{\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil}),\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil})\}
;
or (ii)
a=\mathbf{Right}(q)
and
g\,a=\mathbf{Right}(h^{+}q)
, which means that

a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Right}(h^{+}q)
.
Since
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
it follows that

a\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.
In Case (i),
a=a^{\prime}
and we are done since
a
is defined and hence realises

A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
In Case (ii),
q\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
. Therefore,
by the induction hypothesis,
(h^{+}q)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)

and hence
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.

(
3
) Assume
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
.
We prove the required formula first for compact
c
only and will later show
that this is enough.
Hence we show first

(\forall\hbox{ compact }c)\,(h^{+}c=\mathbf{Amb}(\_,\_)\to(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)).

We prove this by induction on the rank of
c
.
The proof is in large parts similar to the proof of (
2
).
Let
c
be compact and assume
h^{+}c=\mathbf{Amb}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
.
Then
c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
with

f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b=\mathbf{Nil}
,

a^{\prime}=f^{*}a\triangleright g\,a
and

b^{\prime}=f^{*}b\triangleright g\,b
.
Since
f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b=\mathbf{Nil}
, it follows
as in the proof of Statement (
2
) that

a^{\prime}=g\,a\not=\bot
, or

b^{\prime}=g\,b\not=\bot
.
In either case, one of
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
is defined, as required.
It remains to show that every defined element of
\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}
realises

A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
Without restriction let
a^{\prime}
be defined. Hence
a^{\prime}=g\,a

and either (i)
g\,a=a=\mathbf{Left}(p)
, and thus
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Left}(p)

with
p\in\{\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil}),\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil})\}
;
or (ii)
a=\mathbf{Right}(q)
and
g\,a=\mathbf{Right}(h^{+}q)
, which means that

a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Right}(h^{+}q)
.
Since
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
,

\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil})
and
\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil})
both realise
A
.
Hence, in Case (i),
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.
In Case (ii), since
f^{*}a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Nil}
,
q
must be of the
form
\mathbf{Amb}(\_,\_)
. Therefore, since
q
has smaller rank than
c
,
by the induction hypothesis,
(h^{+}q)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)

and hence
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.

To remove the restriction to compact
c
, it
suffices to show that for every
c
there is a compact
c_{0}\sqsubseteq c

such that
h^{+}c_{0}=h^{+}c
.
If
h^{+}c=\bot
, then we can choose
c_{0}=\bot
.
Otherwise,
c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)
.
Since
f^{*}
is continuous and its range contains only compact elements
(namely
\bot
and
\mathbf{Nil}
), there are compact
a_{0}\sqsubseteq a

and
b_{0}\sqsubseteq b
such that
f^{*}a_{0}=f^{*}a
and
f^{*}b_{0}=f^{*}b
.
Therefore, it suffices to show

(\forall\hbox{ compact }a_{0},b_{0})\ (\forall a,b)\,(a_{0}\sqsubseteq a\land b_{0}\sqsubseteq b\land f^{*}(a_{0})=f^{*}a\land f^{*}b_{0}=f^{*}b\\
\to h^{+}\mathbf{Amb}(a_{0},b_{0})=h^{+}\mathbf{Amb}(a,b).

This can be easily shown by induction on the maximum of the ranks of

a_{0}
and
b_{0}
.

Now, we are able to derive the result on Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro
)
we are aiming for.

Lemma 14
.

Rule
(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro)

is realised by
h^{+}
.

Proof 5.4
.

Set
A\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}A_{0}\lor A_{1}
, and assume that
c
realises
B\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)

and
\mathbf{H}(\neg B\to A)
holds.
The former means that
\mathbf{H}(B)\to c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and
the latter that
\neg\mathbf{H}(B)\to\mathbf{H}(A_{0})\wedge\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
.
We have to show that
h^{+}c
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
.

First, assume
\mathbf{H}(B)
. Then
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and, by
Lemma
13
(
1
),

h^{+}c
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and is therefore defined.

Next, suppose
h^{*}c
is defined.
Hence
c=\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)

and
h^{+}c=\mathbf{Amb}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime}=f^{*}a\triangleright g\,a

and
b^{\prime}=f^{*}b\triangleright g(b)
.
We have to show
(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
Since
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
has the same realisers as
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
,
it is sufficient to derive that
(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
.
That is, it suffices to verify that every defined element of
\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}

realises
A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
. Without restriction assume that
a^{\prime}
is defined.
We do a classical case analysis on
\mathbf{H}(B)
.
If
\mathbf{H}(B)
, then
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
. Therefore,
by Lemma
13
(
2
),

(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and hence
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.
If
\neg\mathbf{H}(B)
, then
\mathbf{H}(A_{0})
and
\mathbf{H}(A_{1})
.
By Lemma
13
(
3
) we thus have that,

(h^{+}c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and with the same argument as above we obtain

a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
.

Similarly, an analogue of the well known introduction rule for the connective
\land
is realisable:

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\quad\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)}\,\,\text{($\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}$-$\land$-intro)}.

Define

f^{*}d\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(\_)\to\mathbf{Nil};\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Amb}(u,v))\to f^{*}u\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}v\},

\displaystyle g\,d\,e\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{Left}(d^{\prime})\to\mathbf{case}\,e\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(e^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Pair}(d^{\prime},e^{\prime}));

\displaystyle\mathbf{Right}(e^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbf{Right}(h_{2}\,d\,e^{\prime\prime})\};

\displaystyle\mathbf{Right}(d^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbf{Right}(h_{1}\,d^{\prime\prime}e)\},

h_{1}\,u\,v\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,u\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Amb}(a,b)\to\\
\mathbf{case}\,f^{*}a\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}b\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Nil}\to\mathbf{Amb}(f^{*}a\triangleright g\,a\,v,f^{*}b\triangleright g\,b\,v)\}\},

h_{2}\,u\,v\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,v\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Amb}(\bar{a},\bar{b})\to\\
\mathbf{case}\,f^{*}\bar{a}\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}\bar{b}\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Nil}\to\mathbf{Amb}(f^{*}\bar{a}\triangleright g\,u\,\bar{a},f^{*}\triangleright g\,u\,\bar{b})\}\}.

Lemma 15
.

1.

d\,\mathbf{r}\,A\land c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)\to(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
.

2.

c_{1}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\land c_{2}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)\to(h_{1}\,c_{1}\,c_{2})\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
.

Proof 5.5
.

Both statements are shown by s.p. induction.

(
1
) Assume that
d\,\mathbf{r}\,A
and
c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)
. Then
c=\mathbf{Amb}(\bar{a},\bar{b})
with
f^{*}\bar{a}\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}\bar{b}=\mathbf{Nil}
. Hence,
h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,c=\mathbf{Amb}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime}=f^{*}\bar{a}\triangleright g\,d\,\bar{a}
and
b^{\prime}=f^{*}\bar{b}\triangleright g\,d\,\bar{b}
. Since
f^{*}\bar{a}\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}\bar{b}=\mathbf{Nil}
, we have that
a^{\prime}\neq\bot
or
b^{\prime}\neq\bot
as required. It remains to show that all defined elements of
\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}
realise
(A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
. Without restriction suppose that
a^{\prime}
is defined. Recall that
d\,\mathbf{r}\,A
. So, we have that (i)
\bar{a}=\mathbf{Left}(p)
with
p\,\mathbf{r}\,B
and
g\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,\mathbf{Left}(p)=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Pair}(d,p))
, or (ii)
\bar{a}=\mathbf{Right}(q)
and
g\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,\bar{a}=\mathbf{Right}(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,q)
. Thus,
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Right}(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,q)
. Because
p\,\mathbf{r}\,B
, it follows in Case (i) that
\bar{a}\,\mathbf{r}\,(B\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B))
. Therefore,
(g\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,\bar{a})\,\mathbf{r}\,((A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B))
, i.e.,
(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
.

In Case (ii) we have
q\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)
. By the induction hypothesis we therefore obtain that
(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,q)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
. Thus,
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,((A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B))
which implies that

(h_{2}\,\mathbf{Left}(d)\,c)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B).

(
2
) Now, suppose that
c_{1}\,\mathbf{r}\,A
and
c_{2}\,\mathbf{r}\,B
. Then
c_{1}=\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
with
f^{*}u\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}v=\mathbf{Nil}
and
h_{1}\,c_{1}\,c_{2}=\mathbf{Amb}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime}=f^{*}u\triangleright g\ u\,c_{2}

and
b^{\prime}=f^{*}v\triangleright g\,v\,c_{2}
. Since
f^{*}u\sqcup_{\mathbf{Nil}}f^{*}v=\mathbf{Nil}
, we have that
a^{\prime}=g\,u\,c_{2}\neq\bot
or
b^{\prime}=g\,v\,c_{2}\neq\bot
, as required. Again, it remains to show that each defined element of
\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}
realises
(A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
. Without restriction assume that
a^{\prime}
is defined. Then
a^{\prime}=g\,u\,c_{2}
and (i)
u=\mathbf{Left}(d)
with

g\,u\,c_{2}=\mathbf{case}\,c_{2}\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(e)\to\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Pair}(d,e));\mathbf{Right}(e^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Right}(h_{2}\,u\,e^{\prime})\},

or (ii)
u=\mathbf{Right}(d^{\prime})
and
g\,u\,c_{2}=\mathbf{Right}(h_{1}\,d^{\prime}\,c_{2})
, i.e.,
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Right}(h_{1}\,d^{\prime}\,c_{2})
.

In Case (i) it follows that either
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Pair}(d,e))
and thus
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,((A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((A\land B))
, or
a^{\prime}=\mathbf{Right}(h_{2}\,d\,e^{\prime})
, from which we obtain with the first statement that again
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,((A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B))
.

In Case (ii) we have that
d^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and hence by the induction hypothesis that
(h_{1}\,d^{\prime}\,c_{2})\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
. Thus,
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,((A\land B)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B))
, that is
(h_{1}\,c_{1}\,c_{2})\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\land B)
.

Corollary 16
.

Rule
(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\land
-intro)
is realised by
h_{1}
.

We extend CFP by the Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro
) and (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\land
-intro
).

Lemma 17
.

The following rules for the iterated concurrency modality are derivable:

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}{A}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-H (
A
Harrop))

\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-emb)

\dfrac{\mathord{\downdownarrows}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb)

\dfrac{A}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\quad A\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-bind)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-dist)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\vee B)}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B))}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\vee
-dist)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\quad A\to B}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\wedge B)}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\wedge\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\wedge
-dist)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\to B)}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\to
-dist)

\dfrac{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-idem).

\rule{0.0pt}{22.76219pt}\dfrac{A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}}\ \ldots\ A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{n}}}{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}\lor\cdots\lor B_{n}}}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
)

Proof 5.6
.

Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-H
) follows by induction. From
A\vee A\to A
we obtain with Rules (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-mon
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-H
) that
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee A)\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
and
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)\to A
, hence
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee A)\to A
.

The Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-emb
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb
)
follow directly from the definition of
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
and Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-mon
).

Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return
) follows directly from the definition of
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}

and the Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-return
).

For Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-bind
)
assume that
A\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})
. We prove by induction that also
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})
, that is, we have to show that
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime}))\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})
, which means that we must demonstrate that
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime}))\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A^{\prime}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime}))
. Because of the monotonicity of
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
it suffices to prove that
A\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})\to A^{\prime}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})
, which is an immediate consequence of our assumption.

In case of Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-dist
) we apply the induction
principle again. It suffices to show

\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
.
With (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return
) we have
A\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
and
hence, because of (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
),

A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
.
Therefore,

(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
,
from which it follows with
Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
–mon
) that

\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\to\mathord{\downdownarrows}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})
.
With Rules (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-absorb
) and (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb
) we get

\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B})\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B}
.

The Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\lor
-dist
), (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
),
(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\land
-dist
), (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\to
-dist
), and (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-idem
)
follow from the monadic laws (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return
)
and (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-bind
) in the usual way.

Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
) is obtained, roughly speaking, by iterating Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
). The proof is by induction on
n
.
For
n=1
, the rule follows immediately with Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return
)
and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
).
For the step assume
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}}
,
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{2}}
, …,
A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{n+1}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{2}\lor\cdots\lor B_{n+1}}
.
With Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
) it therefore follows

\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}\lor B_{2}\lor\cdots\lor B_{n+1}}
.
Since
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))
is equivalent to
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)
, we obtain

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}\lor B_{2}\lor\cdots\lor B_{n+1}}
by applying Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
).

The subsequent list contains realisers for the rules in the above lemma extracted from their proofs.

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)}{(\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,\mathbf{Left}\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-emb)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))}{(\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,\mathbf{Right}\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb)

\dfrac{a\,\mathbf{r}\,A}{(f_{\mathrm{ret}}\,a)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return,
f_{\mathrm{ret}}
below)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\quad g\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime}))}{(f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,g\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A^{\prime})}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-bind,
f_{\mathrm{bind}}
below)

(

-
\mathord{\restriction}
-dist,

below)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\quad g\,\mathbf{r}\,(A\to B)}{(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,g\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon,
f_{\mathrm{mon}}
below)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\vee B)}{(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\mathbf{mapLR}\,\,f_{\mathrm{ret}})\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B))}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\vee
-dist)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\wedge B)}{\mathbf{Pair}(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\pi_{L}\,c),f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\pi_{R}\,c))\,\,\mathbf{r}\,(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\wedge\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B))}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\wedge
-dist)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A\to B)}{(f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(\lambda a.\,f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(\lambda f.\,f\,a)\,c))\,\,\mathbf{r}\,(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(B))}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\to
-dist)

\dfrac{c\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A))}{(f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(\lambda a.\,a)\,c)\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-idem)

\dfrac{b_{1}\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}}\,\ldots\,b_{n}\,\mathbf{r}\,A\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{n}}}{(f_{n}\,b_{1}\,\ldots\,b_{n})\,\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{B_{1}\lor\cdots\lor B_{n}}}

(
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
)

where

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ret}}\,a\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}(a),\bot)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,g\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,(\lambda d.\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to\mathbf{Right}(g\,a);\mathbf{Right}(c^{\prime})\to f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,g\,c^{\prime}\}),

\displaystyle f_{\mathord{\restriction}{-}\mathrm{dist}}\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,(\lambda d.\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to d;\mathbf{Right}(c^{\prime})\to f_{\mathord{\restriction}{-}\mathrm{dist}}\,c^{\prime}\}),

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,g\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,(\lambda d.\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to\mathbf{Left}(g\,a);\mathbf{Right}(c^{\prime})\to f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,g\,c^{\prime}\}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{mapLR}\,\,g\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda c.\,\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to\mathbf{Left}(g\,a);\mathbf{Right}(b)\to\mathbf{Right}(g\,b)\},

\displaystyle\pi_{L}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda p.\,\mathbf{case}\,p\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Pair}(a,\_)\to a\},

\displaystyle\pi_{R}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda p.\,\mathbf{case}\,p\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Pair}(\_,b)\to b\},

\displaystyle f_{1}\,b_{1}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}f_{\mathrm{ret}}\mathopen{\downarrow}b_{1},

\displaystyle f_{n+1}\,b_{1}\ldots b_{n+1}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\bar{g}\,b_{1}\,(f_{n}\,b_{2}\,\ldots\,b_{n+1}),

\displaystyle\bar{g}\,a\,c\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Amb}_{\mathbf{LR}}(\mathbf{Left}\mathopen{\downarrow}a,\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}c).

A further useful rule that we will use in the sequel is a concurrent version of half-strong co-induction.

Lemma 18

(
Concurrent Half-strong Co-induction Principle).

Let
\Phi_{0}\colon\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(X)
be a monotone operator and
\Phi(Y)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\Phi_{0}(Y))
. Then:

If
Y\subseteq\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\Phi(Y)\cup\nu\Phi)
then
Y\subseteq\nu\Phi
.

The principle is an immediate consequence of the generalised half-strong co-induction principle (Lemma
8
): Because of Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
)
\Phi
is monotone and with Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-idem
) we have that
\Phi
absorbes
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
.
Note that
\tau(\Phi)(\alpha)
is of the form
\mathbf{A}^{*}(\rho(\alpha))
. So,
if
s:P\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\Phi(P)+\nu\Phi)

realises
P\subseteq\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\Phi(P)\cup\nu\Phi)
,
then
P\subseteq\nu\Phi
is realised by

\mathbf{chscoit}_{\Phi}\,s:P\to\nu\Phi

with

\mathbf{chscoit}_{\Phi}\,s\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}f,

where
f
is defined as in the case of the realisability of generalised half-strong co-induction (Example
4.4
).
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{absorb}^{\alpha}_{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}},\Phi}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(\lambda a.\,a)\colon\mathbf{A}^{*}(\Phi(\alpha))\to\Phi(\alpha),

\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}_{\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}f_{\mathrm{mon}}.

6.
Concurrent Archimedean induction

A powerful tool in the investigation in
[
BT21a
,
BT21b
]
of the relationship between
the signed digit representation and infinite Gray code
is Archimedean induction, which is the Archimedean principle formulated as an induction rule:

\dfrac{(\forall x\neq 0)\ (|x|\leq 1/2\to P(2x))\to P(x)}{(\forall x\neq 0)\ P(x)}\,(\mathrm{AI})

In IFP Rule(AI) is deduced as a special case of well-founded induction (cf.
[
BT21a
]
). A useful variant is:

\dfrac{(\forall x\in B\setminus\{0\})\,P(x)\vee(|x|\leq 1/2\wedge B(2x)\wedge(P(2x)\to P(x)))}{(\forall x\in B\setminus\{0\})\,P(x)}\,(\mathrm{AIB})

In what follows a concurrent version of the Rule (AIB) is needed.

{defi}

Iterated concurrent Archimedean induction
is the rule

\dfrac{(\forall x\in B\setminus\{0\})\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)\vee(|x|\leq 1/2\wedge B(2x)\wedge(P(2x)\to P(x))))}{(\forall x\in B\setminus\{0\})\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))}\,(\mathrm{CAIB^{*}})

where
B
and
P
are non-Harrop predicates.

Lemma 19
.

Rule
(CAIB
∗
)
is realisable.
Let
s
realise the premise of
(CAIB
∗
)
.
Then
\mathbf{caibs}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda b.\,a\,(s\,b)
realises the
conclusion of
(CAIB
∗
)
,
where
a
is defined by simultaneous recursion together with
s^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle a\,w

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,s^{\prime}\,w

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\,u

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{case}\,u\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\

\displaystyle\hskip-45.52458pt\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Left}(c))\to\mathbf{Left}(c);

\displaystyle\hskip-45.52458pt\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(b,d)))\to\mathbf{Right}(f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(f_{\mathrm{ret}}\circ d)\,(a\,(s\,b)));

\displaystyle\hskip-45.52458pt\mathbf{Right}(w^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Right}(a\,w^{\prime})\ \}.

Proof 6.1
.

\lambda b.\,a\,(s\,b)
has the right type,

\tau(B)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\tau(P))

where
\mathbf{A}^{*}(\alpha)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{fix}\,\beta.\,\mathbf{A}(\alpha+\beta)
.
This holds, since we have that

s:\tau(B)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\rho)
,
with
\rho\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\tau(P)+\tau(B)\times(\tau(P)\to\tau(P))
,
from which one can infer by a simple type inference that

a:\mathbf{A}^{*}(\rho)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\tau(P))
, and

s^{\prime}:(\rho+\mathbf{A}^{*}(\rho))\to(\tau(P)+\mathbf{A}^{*}(\tau(P)))
.

Set
C(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}P(x)\vee(|x|\leq 1/2\wedge B(2x)\wedge(P(2x)\to P(x)))
.
To complete the proof, it clearly suffices to show that if
x\neq 0
and

w
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x))
, then
a\,w
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))
.

Let

Q(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\forall w)\,(w\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x))\to(a\,w)\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))).

We use Rule (AI) to prove that
(\forall x\not=0)\,Q(x)
.

Let
x\not=0
. The Archimedean induction hypothesis is

(AIH):

|x|\leq 1/2\to Q(2x)
.

We show
Q(x)
by a side induction on the definition of
w\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x))
.
Hence we assume
w\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x))
, that is,

w\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(C(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x)))
, and have to derive
that
a\,w
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))
.

Thus,
w=\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
and

a\,w=\mathbf{Amb}(s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u,s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}\,v)
.
Furthermore,
u\not=\bot
or
v\not=\bot
, hence

s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u=s^{\prime}\,u
or
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}v=s^{\prime}\,v
.
Moreover, for
k\in\{u,v\}
, if
k\not=\bot
then

k\,\mathbf{r}\,(C(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x)))\,.

Before showing that
\mathbf{Amb}(s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u,s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}v)
realises

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))
, we prove:

If
k\in\{u,v\}
such that
k\not=\bot
, then

(s^{\prime}\,k)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.

(5)

Without restriction assume
k=u\not=\bot
.
Hence
u\,\mathbf{r}\,(C(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x)))

and
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u=s^{\prime}\,u
.

If
u=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Left}(c))
with
c\,\mathbf{r}\,P(x)
, then
s^{\prime}\,u=\mathbf{Left}(c)
, which means that
(s^{\prime}\,u)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.

If
u=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(b,d)))
with
|x|\leq 1/2
,
b\,\mathbf{r}\,B(2x)
and

d\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(2x)\to P(x))
, then
s^{\prime}\,u=\mathbf{Right}(f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(f_{\mathrm{ret}}\circ d)\,(a\,(s\,b)))
.
Moreover, by (AIH),
a\,(s\,b)
realises
P(2x)
. Therefore, since
f_{\mathrm{ret}}\circ d
realises
(P(2x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
, we have that

f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(f_{\mathrm{ret}}\circ d)\,(a\,(s\,b))
realises
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))
, that is,
(s^{\prime}\,u)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.

If
u=\mathbf{Right}(w^{\prime})
with
w^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(C(x))
, then
s^{\prime}\,u=\mathbf{Right}(a\,w^{\prime})
.
By the side induction hypothesis,
(a\,w^{\prime})\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x))
. Thus,
(s^{\prime}\,u)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\lor\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.

From the proof of (
5
) it follows easily that
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u,s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}v\in(\tau(P)+\mathbf{A}^{*}(\tau(P)))
.
Therefore, it remains to show:

1.

For some
k\in\{u,v\}
,
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}k\not=\bot
.

2.

If
k\in\{u,v\}
such that
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}k\not=\bot
,
then
(s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}k)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.

For (
1
) assume without restriction that
k=u\not=\bot
.
Then
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}u=s^{\prime}\,u
. By (
5
),
(s^{\prime}\,u)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
.
Hence,
s^{\prime}\,u\not=\bot
.

To prove (
2
), suppose
s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}k\not=\bot
.
Then
k\not=\bot
. It follows that
(s^{\prime}\mathopen{\downarrow}k)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(x)\vee\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P(x)))
,
by (
5
).

For what follows, we extend CFP again by adding Rule (CAIB
∗
).

7.
Concurrent signed digit and Gray codes

We first briefly formalise the definitions given in Section
3
in IFP and CFP, respectively. For details the reader is referred to
[
BT21a
,
BT21b
]
.

Define the IFP predicates
\mathbf{SD}(x)
and
\mathbf{S}(x)
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{SD}(z)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(z=-1\vee z=1)\vee z=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}|2x-z|\leq 1,

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}(\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}(2x-z).

For
\mathbf{3}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1})+\mathbf{1}
and
\delta^{\omega}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{fix}\,\alpha.\,\delta\times\alpha
, their types are

\tau(\mathbf{SD})=\mathbf{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau(\mathbf{S})=\mathbf{3}^{\omega}.

The predicate
\mathbf{SD}(z)
is realised as follows

\displaystyle d\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{SD}(z)=\mbox{}

\displaystyle(d=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil}))\wedge z=-1)\vee\mbox{}

\displaystyle(d=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil}))\wedge z=1)\vee\mbox{}

\displaystyle(d=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil})\wedge z=0).

In the sequel the three digits
-1,1,0
will be identified with their realisers, which are programs of type
\mathbf{3}
.
For the predicate
\mathbf{S}(x)
we obtain

\displaystyle p\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\nu}{=}(\exists d,p^{\prime})\,p=\mathbf{Pair}(d,p^{\prime})\wedge(\exists z)\,d\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge p^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}(2x-z)

\displaystyle\overset{\nu}{=}(\exists d,p^{\prime})\,p=\mathbf{Pair}(d,p^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(d,x)\leq 1\wedge p^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}(2x-d).

The realisers of
\mathbf{S}(x)
are hence streams of digits -1, 0, 1.

Let us next consider the Gray code case. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}x\not=0\to\mathbf{B}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}1-2|x|,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}(-1\leq x\leq 1)\wedge\mathbf{D}(x)\wedge\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{t}(x)),

where types are
\tau(\mathbf{B})=\tau(\mathbf{D})=\mathbf{2}
with
\mathbf{2}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}
, and
\tau(\mathbf{G})=\mathbf{2}^{\omega}
. Then the predicates
\mathbf{D}(x)
and
\mathbf{G}(x)
are realised as follows

\displaystyle a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{D}(x)=a:\mathbf{2}\wedge(x\not=0\to(a=\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Nil})\wedge x\leq 0)\vee(a=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Nil})\wedge x\geq 0),

\displaystyle q\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{G}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}(-1\leq x\leq 1)\wedge(\exists a,q^{\prime})\,q=\mathbf{Pair}(a,q^{\prime})\wedge a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{D}(x)\wedge q^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{t}(x)).

Since an infinite Gray code may contain a
\bot
, a sequential access of the sequence from left to right will diverge when it accesses a
\bot
. However, because at most one
\bot
is contained in each sequence, if one evaluates the first two cells concurrently, then at least one of the two processes is guaranteed to terminate. On the basis of this idea Berger and Tsuiki
[
BT21b
]
showed that a concurrent algorithm converting infinite Gray code into signed digit representation can be extracted from a CFP proof. To this end a concurrent variant of the predicate
\mathbf{S}
is introduced

\mathbf{S}_{2}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathord{\downdownarrows}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}_{2}(2x-z)).

\mathbf{S}_{2}(x)
means that a signed digit representation of
x
is obtained through the concurrent computation of two threads. Note that
\tau(\mathbf{S}_{2})=\mathbf{fix}\,\alpha.\,\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{3}\times\alpha)
.

{thmC}
[
[
BT21b
]
]

\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{G}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
.

This result expresses the fact that, as explained above, when computably translating from Gray code to signed digit representation one needs to allow for computations to be carried out concurrently, which, however is not the case for the converse translation from signed digit representation to Gray code. On the other hand the result is not completely satisfying, as one would like to see under which conditions both representations are computably equivalent.

To achieve a result of this kind we have to introduce concurrent Gray code. In addition we have to allow for iterated concurrent computations. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{*}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-z)),

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{*}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0),

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}x\not=0\to\mathbf{B}^{*}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\overset{\nu}{=}(-1\leq x\leq 1)\wedge\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x)).

For the concurrent signed digit representation we have

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\nu(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})
where

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}(Y)(x)=\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\land Y(2x-z)),

\displaystyle\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})(\alpha)=\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{3}\times\alpha),

\displaystyle\tau(\mathbf{S}^{*})=\mathbf{fix}\,\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})=\mathbf{fix}\,\,\alpha\,.\,\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{3}\times\alpha),

\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}:(\alpha\to\beta)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{3}\times\alpha)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{3}\times\beta),
