\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}\,f=f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\lambda\mathbf{Pair}(d,a)\,.\,\mathbf{Pair}(d,f\,a)),

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{*}(\alpha)=\mathbf{fix}\,\beta\,.\,\mathbf{A}(\alpha+\beta),

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{mon}}:(\alpha\to\beta)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\alpha)\to\mathbf{A}^{*}(\beta),

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{mon}}\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\lambda h.\,\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,(\lambda d\,.\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to\mathbf{Left}(h\,a);\,\mathbf{Right}(c)\to f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,h\,c\}).

On the other hand, for the concurrent infinite Gray code we have

\mathbf{G}^{*}=\nu(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})
where

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(Y)(x)=\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\land Y(\mathbf{t}(x)),

\displaystyle\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})(\alpha)=\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{2})\times\alpha,

\displaystyle\tau(\mathbf{G}^{*})=\mathbf{fix}\,\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})=\mathbf{fix}\,\,\alpha\,.\,\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{2})\times\alpha=(\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{2}))^{\omega},

\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})}:(\alpha\to\beta)\to(\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{2})\times\alpha)\to(\mathbf{A}^{*}(\mathbf{2})\times\beta),

\displaystyle\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}\,f\,\mathbf{Pair}(m,a)=\mathbf{Pair}(m,f\,a).

We see that a realiser of
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
is simply an ordinary infinite stream
(where the cons-operation is the deterministic constructor
\mathbf{Pair}
) of non-deterministic
partial binary digits, whereas a realiser of
\mathbf{S}^{*}(x)
is something that could be called
a non-deterministic stream (where the cons-operation is non-deterministic)
given by a pair of concurrent computations at least one
of which will yield a head, which is signed digit, and a tail, which is again a
non-deterministic stream. Consequently, the function
\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})}

is much simpler than
\mathbf{mon}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}
.

Our next goal is to show that
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*}
. Note that iterated concurrent computations also occur in the case of
\mathbf{S}_{2}
, which can be seen by unfolding the co-inductive definition.

Lemma 20
.

If
\mathbf{S}^{*}(x)
, then also

1.

\mathbf{S}^{*}(-x)
and

2.

\mathbf{S}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
.

Proof 7.1
.

(
1
)
Let
P\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\mid\mathbf{S}^{*}(-x)\,\}
. We use co-indution to prove that
P\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{*}
. So, we have to show that

\mathbf{S}^{*}(-x)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-(2x-z^{\prime}))),

i.e.,

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,-x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-2x-z))\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-(2x-z^{\prime}))).

Because of Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
) it suffices to prove that

(\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,-x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-2x-z)\rightarrow(\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-(2x-z^{\prime})).

Let
z\in\mathbf{SD}
. We show that

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,-x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-2x-z)\rightarrow(\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(-(2x-z^{\prime})).

If
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,-x)
with
\mathbf{S}^{*}(-2x-z)
, then
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(-z,x)
. Moreover,
\mathbf{S}^{*}(-(2x-z^{\prime}))
with
z^{\prime}=-z
.

(
2
)
Let
\mathbf{Q}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,y\mid(\exists x)\,\mathbf{S}^{*}(x)\wedge y=\mathbf{t}(x)\,\}
. We use concurrent half-strong co-induction (Lemma
18
) to show that
\mathbf{Q}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{*}
. This means that we have to prove that

\mathbf{Q}(y)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},y)\wedge\mathbf{Q}(2y-z^{\prime}))\vee\mathbf{S}^{*}(y)).

By the definition of
\mathbf{Q}
we therefore have to show for
x,y
with
y=\mathbf{t}(x)
that

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-z))\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},y)\wedge\mathbf{Q}(2y-z^{\prime}))\vee\mathbf{S}^{*}(y)).

Because of the monotonicity and return rules for
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
, it suffices to prove that for
x,y,
with
y=\mathbf{t}(x)
and
z\in\mathbf{SD}
that

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-z)\rightarrow((\exists z^{\prime})\,\mathbf{SD}(z^{\prime})\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z^{\prime},y)\wedge\mathbf{Q}(2y-z^{\prime}))\vee\mathbf{S}^{*}(y),

which follows by case distinction:

Case

z=-1

We have that
x\in[-1,0]
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x+1)
. Since
2x+1=\mathbf{t}(x)=y
in this case, it follows that
\mathbf{S}^{*}(y)
.

Case

z=1
.

Now,
x\in[0,1]
. Therefore,
2x-1=-\mathbf{t}(x)=-y
. Since moreover,
\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-1)
, we have that
\mathbf{S}^{*}(-y)
, from which we obtain that
\mathbf{S}^{*}(y)
, by Part (
1
).

Case

z=0
.

It follows that
x\in[-1/2,1/2]
, which implies that
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,\mathbf{t}(x))
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{Q}(2y-1)
. Note that
2y-1=-\mathbf{t}(y)=-\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{t}(x))=\mathbf{t}(2x)
. Since
\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x)
, it follows that
\mathbf{Q}(2y-1)
.

The first statement is realised by

f_{\ref{lem-neg}.\ref{lem-neg-1}}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,\lambda\,\mathbf{Pair}(d,a).\,\mathbf{Pair}({-}d,a),

and the second by

f_{\ref{lem-neg}.\ref{lem-neg-2}}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{chscoit}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}\,(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,\lambda\,\mathbf{Pair}(d,a).\,\\
\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{{-}1\to\mathbf{Right}(a);\\
1\to\mathbf{Right}(f_{\ref{lem-neg}.\ref{lem-neg-1}}\,a);\\
0\to\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}(\mathbf{Pair}(1,a)),\bot))\,\}).

Proposition 21
.

\mathbf{S}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
.

Proof 7.2
.

The proof is by co-induction. Because of Lemma
20
(
2
)
it remains to show that

\mathbf{S}^{*}(x)\rightarrow\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\,,

that is,
(\forall x\in\mathbf{S}^{*}\setminus\{0\})\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x))
.
We prove this formula by iterated concurrent Archimedean induction (CAIB
∗
).
Therefore, we have to show

(\forall x\in\mathbf{S}^{*}\setminus\{0\})\,\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x)\vee(|x|\leq 1/2\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x)\wedge(\mathbf{B}(2x)\to\mathbf{B}(x))))\,.

By Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon) and since
\mathbf{B}(2x)\to\mathbf{B}(x)
holds,
it suffices to show

((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-d))\to(\mathbf{B}(x)\vee(|x|\leq 1/2\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x)))\,.

Let
z\in\mathbf{SD}
with
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x-z)
.
If
z=-1
or
z=1
, then
x\leq 0
or
x\geq 0
, hence
\mathbf{B}(x)
.
If
z=0
, then
|2x|\leq 1
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}(2x)
.

Statement
\mathbf{S}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{*}
is realised by

\displaystyle f_{d}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{caibs}\,(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,\lambda\,\mathbf{Pair}(d,a).\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{{-}

\displaystyle 1\to\mathbf{Left}({-}1);

\displaystyle 1\to\mathbf{Left}(1);

\displaystyle 0\to\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a,\mathbf{id}))\}),

and the inclusion
\mathbf{S}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
by

f_{\ref{prop-ctog}}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{coit}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{G}^{*}})}\,(\lambda\,c.\,\mathbf{Pair}(f_{d}\,c,f_{\ref{lem-neg}.\ref{lem-neg-2}}\,c))
,
that is,

f_{\ref{prop-ctog}}\,c\overset{\mathbf{rec}\,}{=}\mathbf{Pair}(f_{d}\,c,f_{\ref{prop-ctog}}\,(f_{\ref{lem-neg}.\ref{lem-neg-2}}\,c)).

Let us now consider the converse inclusion.

Lemma 22
.

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{*}(-x)
.

Proof 7.3
.

Let
\mathbf{P}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\mid\mathbf{G}^{*}(-x)\,\}
. We will use strong co-induction to show that
\mathbf{P}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
. To this end it needs to be shown that

\mathbf{P}(y)\to\mathbf{D}^{*}(y)\wedge(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{t}(y))\vee\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(y))).

Assume that
\mathbf{P}(y)
. Then
y=-x
for some
x\in\mathbf{G}^{*}
. It follows that
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
. The first property implies that
\mathbf{D}^{*}(-x)
, that is
\mathbf{D}^{*}(y)
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{t}(x)=\mathbf{t}(-x)
, we also have
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(y))
.

The statement
(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathbf{G}^{*}(-x))
is realised by

f_{\ref{lem-gneg}}\,\mathbf{Pair}(m,a)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Pair}(f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\lambda d.\,{-}d)\,m,a).

Lemma 23
.

For
d\in\{-1,1\}
,

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\rightarrow\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(d,x)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-d).

Proof 7.4
.

By case distinction on
d
we show that

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-d).

Therefore, assume that
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
. Then
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
.

Case

d=-1
.

This case is obvious as
\mathbf{t}(x)=2x+1
.

Case

d=1
.

Now
\mathbf{t}(x)=1-2x=-(2x-1)
. Therefore, the statement follows with Lemma
22
.

(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to(\forall d\in\{{-}1,1\})\,(\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(d,x)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-d)))

is realised by

f_{\ref{lem-glikec}}\,\mathbf{Pair}(\_,a)\,d\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{{-}1\to a;1\to f_{\ref{lem-gneg}}\,a\}.

Lemma 24
.

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathbf{G}^{*}(1-x)
.

Proof 7.5
.

Assume
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,x)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
.
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,x)
implies
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,1-x)

and hence
\mathbf{D}^{*}(1-x)
. Therefore, it suffices to show
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(1-x))
.
Note that in our case,
\mathbf{t}(1-x)=2x-1
. Thus,
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
implies

\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(1-x))
, by Lemma
23
.

(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(1,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathbf{G}^{*}(1-x))

is realised by

f_{\ref{lem-min1}}\,a\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Pair}(\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}(1),\bot),f_{\ref{lem-glikec}}\,a\,1).

Hence,

f_{\ref{lem-min1}}\,\mathbf{Pair}(\_,a)=\mathbf{Pair}(\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Left}(1),\bot),f_{\ref{lem-gneg}}\,a).

Lemma 25
.

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(0,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x)
.

Proof 7.6
.

Assume
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(0,x)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
. Then
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
.
Hence also
\mathbf{D}^{*}(2x)
and
\mathbf{t}(x)\geq 0
. With Lemma
24
it
follows
\mathbf{G}^{*}(2|x|)
(since
1-\mathbf{t}(x)=2|x|
).
Therefore,
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(2x))
(since
\mathbf{t}(|x|)=\mathbf{t}(x)
). It follows

\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x)
.

(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(0,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x))

is realised by

f_{\ref{lem-ii0}}\,\mathbf{Pair}(m,a)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{Pair}(m,\pi_{R}\,(f_{\ref{lem-min1}}\,a)).

Lemma 26
.

\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\leftrightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\not=0}
.

Proof 7.7
.

Note that
\neg(x\not=0)
, i.e,
x=0
, implies that
x\leq 0\wedge x\geq 0
. Now, assume that
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
. Then we obtain with Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-intro
) that
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\not=0}
.

For the converse implication assume that
x\not=0
. Then if follows with Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mp
) that
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)
. So,
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
holds.

(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\lor x\geq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\neq 0})

is realised by the function
h^{+}
as defined in Lemma
14
.
The converse implication is realised by the identity.

Lemma 27
.

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x))
.

Proof 7.8
.

Assume
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
.
We have to show
A(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x))
.
By Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb), it suffices to show
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A(x))
.
Therefore, by Rule (
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\vee
-elim
) it is sufficient to derive

1.

A(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\not=0}

2.

A(x)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{t}(x)\not=0}
.

Set
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(y)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(y\leq 0\vee y\geq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{t}(x)\not=0}
.
The assumption
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
entails
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
and
\mathbf{D}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
and
therefore also
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(x)
as well as
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{t}(x))
, by Lemma
26
.
Now, (
1
) follows immediately from
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(x)
,
by Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
) and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
).

For (
2
) we use that
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{t}(x))
. Because of Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
) it suffices to derive

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{t}(x)\leq 0\vee\mathbf{t}(x)\geq 0)\to A(x)
.
With Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-bind) this can be further reduced to showing

(\mathbf{t}(x)\leq 0\vee\mathbf{t}(x)\geq 0)\to A(x)
.
If
\mathbf{t}(x)\leq 0
, then
x\not=0
,
and hence
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)
because of
\mathbf{D}^{*}(x)
.
As
x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0\to(\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)
,
an application of Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
) leads to
A(x)
.
If
\mathbf{t}(x)\geq 0
, then
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(0,x)
. Hence,
A(x)
,
by Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-return
).

(\forall x)\,(\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)))

is realised by the function

f_{\ref{lem-halfC}}\,\mathbf{Pair}(m,\mathbf{Pair}(n,\_))\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,\mathbf{Right}\,\mathbf{Amb}(h^{+}\,m,\\
f_{\mathrm{bind}}\,(\lambda\,c.\,\mathbf{case}\,c\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(a)\to m;\mathbf{Right}(b)\to f_{\mathrm{ret}}\,0\})\,n).

Proposition 28
.

\mathbf{G}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{*}
.

Proof 7.9
.

Again the assertion follows by co-induction. We have to show that

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-z)).

From Lemmas
23
and
25
it follows

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to(\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\to(\exists z)\,(\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-z)),

from which we obtain by Rule (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
) that

\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x))\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,(\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x)\wedge\mathbf{G}^{*}(2x-z))).

Note that because of Lemma
27
the assumption
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists z)\,\mathbf{SD}(z)\wedge\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(z,x))
can be discharged.

The inclusion
\mathbf{G}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{*}
is realised by

f_{\ref{prop-gtoc}}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{coit}_{\tau(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}^{*}})}\,(\lambda a.\,f_{\mathrm{mon}}\,(\lambda d.\,\mathbf{case}\,d\,\mathbf{of}\,\{{-}1\to f_{\ref{lem-glikec}}\,a\,d;\\
1\to f_{\ref{lem-glikec}}\,a\,d;0\to f_{\ref{lem-ii0}}\,a\})\,(f_{\ref{lem-halfC}}\,a)).

Theorem 29
.

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*}

8.
The compact sets case

As is well known, the collection
\mathcal{K}(X)
of non-empty compact subsets of a non-empty compact metric space is a compact space again with respect to the Hausdorff metric
\mu_{\mathrm{H}}
.

{defi}

Let
(X,E)
be a digit space. A
digital tree
is a nonempty set
T\subseteq E^{<\omega}

of finite sequences of digits that is downwards closed under the prefix ordering
and has no maximal element, that is,
[]\in T
and whenever
[e_{0},\ldots,e_{n}]\in T
, then

[e_{0},\ldots,e_{n-1}]\in T
and
[e_{0},\ldots,e_{n},e]\in T
for some
e\in E
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{E}
denote the set of digital trees with digits in
E
.
Note that each such tree is finitely branching as
E
is finite.
Moreover, every element
[e_{0},\ldots,e_{n-1}]\in T
can be continued to an
infinite path
\alpha
in
T
, that is,
\alpha\in E^{\omega}
is such that
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}
, for
i<n
, and

[\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}]\in T
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
In the following we write
\alpha\in[T]
to mean that
\alpha
is a
path in
T
, and by a path we always mean an infinite path.

[T]
is a non-empty compact subset of
E^{\omega}
, for every tree
T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
, and conversely, for every non-empty compact subset
C
of
E^{\omega}
,
C=[T^{C}]
, where
T^{C}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,\alpha^{<n}\mid\alpha\in C\wedge n\in\mathbb{N}\,\}
(cf.
[
BS16
]
).

For
T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
and
n\geq 0
, let
T^{\leq n}
be the finite initial subtree of
T
of height
n
. Then

T^{\leq n}=\{\,\alpha^{<m}\mid\alpha\in[T]\wedge m\leq n\,\}.

Every such initial subtree defines a map
f_{T,n}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}(X)
from
X
into the powerset of
X
in the obvious way:

f_{T,n}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,\vec{e}(x)\mid\vec{e}\in E^{n}\cap T\,\}.

{defi}

For every
T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
we define its
value
by

(\!|T|\!)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\bigcap\nolimits_{n\in\mathbf{N}}f_{T,n}[X].

{lemC}

[
[
BS16
]
]

(\!|T|\!)=\{\,[\![\alpha]\!]\mid\alpha\in[T]\,\}
.

The metric defined on
E^{\omega}
in Section
2
can be transferred to
\mathcal{T}_{E}
. As we will see next, it coincides with the Hausdorff metric.

{lemC}

[
[
BS16
]
]
For
S,T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
,

\delta_{\mathrm{H}}(S,T)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if $S=T$,}\\
2^{-\min\{\,n\mid S^{\leq n}\not=T^{\leq n}\,\}}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

{propC}

[
[
BS16
]
]

1.

(\!|\cdot|\!)\colon\mathcal{T}_{E}\to\mathcal{K}(X)
is onto and uniformly
continuous.

2.

The topology on
\mathcal{K}(X)
induced by the Hausdorff metric is equivalent
to the quotient topology induced by
(\!|\cdot|\!)
.

As a consequence of Lemma
8
we have for trees
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
that

(\!|T_{1}|\!)=(\!|T_{2}|\!)\Longleftrightarrow(\forall\alpha\in[T_{1}])(\exists\beta\in[T_{2}])\,\alpha\sim\beta\wedge(\forall\beta\in[T_{2}])(\exists\alpha\in[T_{1}])\,\alpha\sim\beta.

{defi}

A digital tree
T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
is
full
, if
[T]
is closed under
\sim
.

Lemma 30
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
be full. Then

(\!|T_{1}|\!)=(\!|T_{2}|\!)\Longleftrightarrow T_{1}=T_{2}.

Proof 8.1
.

We have that

\displaystyle(\!|T_{1}|\!)=(\!|T_{2}|\!)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow(\forall\alpha\in[T_{1}])(\exists\beta\in[T_{2}])\,\alpha\sim\beta\wedge(\forall\beta\in[T_{2}])(\exists\alpha\in[T_{1}])\,\alpha\sim\beta

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[T_{1}]\subseteq[T_{2}]\wedge[T_{2}]\subseteq[T_{1}]\qquad\text{(as $T_{1},T_{2}$ are full)}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow T_{1}=T_{2}.

The converse implication holds trivially, as
(\!|\cdot|\!)
is a map.

Lemma 31
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
and
C
be a non-empty compact subset of
E^{\omega}
. Then the following two statements hold:

1.

If
T
is full, then
[T]
is a non-empty compact subset of
E^{\omega}
that is closed under
\sim
.

2.

If
C
is closed under
\sim
, then
T^{C}
is full.

By Proposition
1
,
\widehat{[\![\cdot]\!]}
is a bijection between
E^{\omega}/\mathord{\sim}
and space
X
. So, if
C
is a non-empty compact, and hence closed, subset of
X
,
\widehat{[\![\cdot]\!]}^{-1}[C]
is a non-empty closed subset of
E^{\omega}/\mathord{\sim}
. Consequently,
\overline{C}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}q_{\sim}^{-1}[\widehat{[\![\cdot]\!]}^{-1}[C]]
is a non-empty closed, and thus compact, subset of
E^{\omega}
, which in addition is closed under
\sim
. It follows that
T^{\overline{C}}
is a full tree in
\mathcal{T}_{D}
with
[\![T^{\overline{C}}]\!]=C
.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{f}_{E}
be the subspace of full trees in
\mathcal{T}_{E}
.

Proposition 32
.

(\!|\cdot|\!)\colon\mathcal{T}^{f}_{E}\to\mathcal{K}(X)
is one-to-one and onto.

This shows that
\mathcal{K}(X)
can be represented in straightforward one-to-one way without requiring that
X
is represented in this way. For the special case of the real interval
\mathbb{I}
and Gray code we have seen in Section
2
that
[\![\cdot]\!]\colon\widehat{G}\to\mathbb{I}
is one-to-one. Hence, every digital tree
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\overline{\mathrm{GF}}}
with
[T]\subseteq\widehat{G}
is full.

9.
Archimedean induction for compact sets

Archimedean induction is a formulation of the Archimedean property as an induction principle introduced in
[
BT21a
]
. It turned out quite a powerful proof tool. We will now lift this induction principle to the case of non-empty compact sets. Let
\mathbf{Z}(x)
be the predicate stating that
x
is an integer. Moreover, for
K:\mathcal{P}(\iota)
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
define

\displaystyle K\leq 0\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\forall x\mathrel{\varepsilon}K)\,x\leq 0,

\displaystyle K\geq 0\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\forall x\mathrel{\varepsilon}K)\,x\geq 0,

\displaystyle|K|\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,y\mid(\exists x\mathrel{\varepsilon}K)\,y=|x|\,\},

\displaystyle nK\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,y\mid(\exists x\mathrel{\varepsilon}K)\,y=nx\,\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{0}(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{K}(K)\wedge 0\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}K.

Here,
\mathbf{K}
is a predicate constant denoting the set of non-empty compact subsets of
the compact interval

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}[-1,1]
(see Section
4.1
).

{defi}

Archimedean induction for compact sets
is the following rule

\frac{(\forall K\in\mathbf{K}_{0})\,((\forall K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\wedge|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\rightarrow P(2K^{\prime}))\rightarrow P(K))}{(\forall K\in\mathbf{K}_{0})\,P(K)}\,\,\mathrm{(AIC)}.

Also Archimedean induction for compact sets is a special case of well-founded induction. Set

K^{\prime\prime}\prec K\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}K\in\mathbf{K}\wedge(\exists K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})\,(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\wedge|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\wedge K^{\prime\prime}=2K^{\prime}).

Then the premise of Rule (AIC) is equivalent to
\textbf{Prog}_{\prec,\mathbf{K}_{0}}(P)
.

Lemma 33
.

\mathbf{Acc}_{\prec}(K)
if and only if
K\in\mathbf{K}_{0}
.

Proof 9.1
.

The ‘only if’ part follows by induction on
\mathbf{Acc}_{\prec}(K)
.
Since
\mathbf{Acc}_{\prec}=\mu\Phi
with

\displaystyle\Phi(X)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\in\mathbf{K}\mid(\forall K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})\,(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\land|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\to X(2K^{\prime}))\,\}

we have to show that
\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{0})\subseteq\mathbf{K}_{0}
. Let
K\in\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{0})
and
suppose that
0\mathrel{\varepsilon}K
. Then the compact set
\{0\}
is a subset of
K

and
|\{0\}|\leq 1/2
. Since
K\in\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{0})
, it follows that

2\{0\}\in\mathbf{K}_{0}
,
which is a contradiction.

The ‘if’ part reduces, by
BT
nc
, to the implication
K\in\mathbf{K}_{0}\to\neg\path\_{\prec}(K)$.Therefore,weassume$K\in\bK\_{0}
and
\path\_{\prec}(K)$withtheaimtoarriveatacontradiction.Recallthat\begin{align*}\path\_{\prec}(K)&\overset{\nu}{=}K\in\mathbf{K}\wedge(\exists K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})\,(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\wedge|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\wedge\path\_{\prec}(2K')).\end{align*}Hencebyunfolding$\path\_{\prec}(K)
we can construct a decreasing
sequence
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\subseteq\mathbf{K}
such that
K_{0}=K
and
for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
|K_{n}|\leq 2^{-n}
.

The sequence
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
is constructed such that
K_{0}=K

and for all
n
,
\path\_{\prec}(2^nK\_n)
,
|K_{n}|\leq 2^{-n}
, and
K_{n+1}\subseteq K_{n}
.
For
K_{0}
the properties hold by assumption. For the step, we use that

\path\_{\prec}(2^nK\_n)
holds and therefore exists
K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K}
such that

K^{\prime}\subseteq 2^{n}K_{n}
,
|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2
and
\path\_{\prec}(2K')$.Weset$K\_{n+1}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}2^{-n}K^{\prime}
. Since
2^{n+1}K_{n+1}=2K^{\prime}
it follows that

\path\_{\prec}(2^{n+1}K\_{n+1})
holds.
Furthermore,
|K_{n+1}|=2^{-n}|K^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-(n+1)}
.
Finally,
K_{n+1}=2^{-n}K^{\prime}\subseteq 2^{-n}(2^{n}K_{n})=K_{n}
.

Since
K
is compact, there exists

x\mathrel{\varepsilon}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbf{N}}K_{n}
. Then
|x|\leq 2^{-n}
, for all

n\in\mathbf{N}
. By the Archimedean axiom,
x=0
, hence
0\mathrel{\varepsilon}K
,
contradicting our assumption.

Proposition 34
.

Archimedean induction for compact sets
(AIC)
is derivable in
IFP(
\mathcal{A}_{R}
)
and realised by
\mathbf{rec}\,
.

Proof 9.2
.

It remains to show the second statement. Note that both
\prec
and the predicate
\mathbf{K}_{0}(K)
are Harrop. Moreover, let
s
realise the premise of Rule (AIC). Then
s
also realises
\textbf{Prog}_{\prec,\mathbf{K}_{0}}
. Therefore, it follows with the result in Example
4.4
that
\mathbf{rec}\,\,s
realises
\mathbf{Acc}_{\prec}\cap\mathbf{K}_{0}\subseteq P
which is equivalent to the conclusion of the rule.

In applications, Archimedean induction is mostly used for compact sets that are generated in a particular way and therefore come with a special kind of realisers. Here, we are interested in the case that non-empty compact sets are represented by signed digit code.

{defi}

We define the analogue of the signed digit representation for compact sets as

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathbf{K}(K)\land(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\wedge(\forall d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))

with
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\mid\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(d,x)\,\}
,

\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\mid(\exists d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)\,\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}(d,x)\,\}
,

K_{d}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}K\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}
, and
\mathbf{av}_{d}(x)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(x+d)/2
.

As follows from the definition of realisability, the type
\tau(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K))

of realisers of the formula
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
is given by

\displaystyle\tau(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{fix}\,\alpha.\,\sum\nolimits_{E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})}\alpha^{\|E\|}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{fix}\,\alpha.\,\{-1\}\times\alpha+\{0\}\times\alpha+\{1\}\times\alpha+\{-1,0\}\times\alpha^{2}+\mbox{}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\{-1,1\}\times\alpha^{2}+\{0,1\}\times\alpha^{2}+\{-1,0,1\}\times\alpha^{3},

which is essentially the set
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{SD}}
of all digital trees.

In the case of non-empty compact sets with property
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
the Archimedean induction rule
can be much simplified. Let
\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\mathbf{K}}
denote the set of all
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
with
0\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}K
.

{defi}

Archimedean induction for signed-digit represented compact sets
is the rule

\frac{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\mathbf{K}})\,(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K))))}{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\mathbf{K}})\,P(K)}\,\,\mathrm{(AICSD)}

where
P
is a non-Harrop predicate.

Proposition 35
.

Archimedean induction for signed-digit represented compact sets
(AICSD)
is derivable in
IFP(
\mathcal{A}_{R}
)
, and if
s
realises the premise, then

f\,a\overset{\rm rec}{=}\mathbf{case}\,s\,a\,\mathbf{of}\{\mathbf{Left}(b)\rightarrow b;\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},h))\rightarrow h(fa^{\prime})\}

realises the conclusion.

Proof 9.3
.

We will show that Rule (AICSD) is a consequence of Rule (AIC). Set
A(X)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(X)\rightarrow P(X)
. It suffices to show that the premise of (AICSD) implies the premise of (AIC). Therefore, let
K\in\mathbf{K}_{0}
and assume that

(\forall K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\wedge|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\rightarrow A(2K^{\prime}))

(6)

We have to prove that
A(K)
. So, let
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
. Then we need to derive
P(K)
.

By the premise of (AICSD) we have that

1.

P(K)
or

2.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K)).

In the first case we are done. Let us therefore consider the second case.

Since
|K_{0}|\leq 1/2
, by (
6
),
A(2(K_{0}))
holds, i.e.,

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(2(K_{0})).

Since we know that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
, we obtain that
P(2(K_{0}))
and hence,
as we are considering the second case, that
P(K)
.

As we have just seen, the premise of (AICSD) implies the premise of (AIC). If the first premise is realised by
s
the latter is realised by

s^{\prime}=\lambda f.\,\lambda a.\,\mathbf{case}\,s\,a\,\mathbf{of}\{\mathbf{Left}(b)\rightarrow b;\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},h))\rightarrow h\,(f\,a^{\prime})\}.

Thus,
f\overset{\rm rec}{=}s^{\prime}\,f
, i.e.,

f\,a\overset{\rm rec}{=}\mathbf{case}\,s\,a\,\mathbf{of}\{\mathbf{Left}(b)\rightarrow b;\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},h))\rightarrow h\,(f\,a^{\prime})\},

realises the conclusion
(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\mathbf{K}})\,P(K)
.

If one strengthens the premise of Rule (AICSD) to all
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
instead of only those not containing
0
, one can strengthen the conclusion to a restriction.

{defi}

Archimedean induction with restriction for signed-digit represented compact sets
is the rule

\frac{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}})\,(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K))))}{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}})\,P(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{0\,\mathrel{\not\varepsilon}\,K}}\,\,\text{(AICR)}

where
P
is a productive non-Harrop predicate.

Proposition 36
.

Archimedean induction with restriction for signed-digit represented compact sets
(AICR)
is realisable. More precisely, if
s
realises the premise, then the conclusion is realised by

\chi\,a\overset{\rm rec}{=}\mathbf{case}\,s\,a\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(b)\to b;\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},f))\to f\mathopen{\downarrow}(\chi\,a^{\prime})\}.

Proof 9.4
.

Assuming
a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
we have to show

1.

0\,\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}K\to\chi\,a\not=\bot

2.

\chi\,a\not=\bot\to(\chi\,a)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
.

(
1
) It suffices to show

(\forall K\in\mathbf{K}_{0})(\forall a)\,(a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow\chi\,a\not=\bot).

We prove the statement by Archimedean induction for compact sets. Let
K\in\mathbf{K}_{0}
and assume, as induction hypothesis,

(\forall K^{\prime}\in\mathbf{K})(K^{\prime}\subseteq K\wedge|K^{\prime}|\leq 1/2\rightarrow(\forall a^{\prime})(a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2K^{\prime})\rightarrow\chi\,a^{\prime}\not=\bot)).

We need to show that
(\forall a)(a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow\chi\,a\not=\bot)
. Assume
a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. Then

(s\,a)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K)))).

If
s\,a=\mathbf{Left}(b)
where
b\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
, then
\chi\,a=b
. Since
P(K)
is productive, by asumption,
b\not=\bot
. Hence,
\chi\,a\not=\bot
.
If, however,
s\,a=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},f))
, then

a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
(with
K_{0}\in\mathbf{K}
) and

f\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K))
.
Since
|K_{0}|\leq 1/2
, we have
\chi\,a^{\prime}\not=\bot
, by the induction
hypothesis. It follows that
\chi\,a=f\mathopen{\downarrow}(\chi\,a^{\prime})\not=\bot
. Thus, we are done.

(
2
) We use Scott induction, that is, we consider the approximations
\chi_{i}
of
\chi
,

\displaystyle\chi_{0}\,a=\bot,

\displaystyle\chi_{i+1}\,a=\mathbf{case}\,s\,a\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(b)\to b;\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},f))\to f\mathopen{\downarrow}(\chi_{i}\,a)\}

Observe that a restricted form of Scott induction (as is used here) is included in the axiom set for the extension RIFP of IFP that allows to deal with realisability in a formal way (cf.
[
BT21a
]
).

By the continuity of function application, if
\chi\,a\not=\bot
, then
\chi_{i}\,a\not=\bot
, for some
i\in\mathbf{N}
. Therefore, it suffices to show

(\forall i\in\mathbf{N})(a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\wedge\chi_{i}\,a\not=\bot\rightarrow(\chi\,a)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)).

We induce on
i
. The induction base is trivial as
\chi_{0}\,a=\bot
. For the induction step assume
a\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
and
\chi_{i+1}\,a\not=\bot
. Then

(s\,a)\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K)))).

If
s\,a=\mathbf{Left}(b)
where
b\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
, then
\chi\,a=b
and we are done. In the other case
s\,a=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(a^{\prime},f))
where
a^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
and
f\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K))
. Then
\chi_{i+1}\,a=f\mathopen{\downarrow}(\chi_{i}\,a^{\prime})
. Since
\chi_{i+1}\,a\not=\bot
and the application of
f
is strict, it follows that
\chi_{i}\,a^{\prime}\not=\bot
as well. By the induction hypothesis we therefore have that
(\chi\,a^{\prime})\,\mathbf{r}\,P(2(K_{0}))
. Consequently,
\chi\,a=(f\mathopen{\downarrow}(\chi\,a^{\prime}))\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
.

As in the real number case, in what follows also a concurrent version of the predicate
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
for the signed digit representation of non-empty compact subsets of the interval
\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}
will be considered.

{defi}

\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathbf{K}(K)\land\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\wedge(\forall d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))).

In this case the above induction rule is still valid, if we allow the ‘or’ in the premise being decided concurrently.

{defi}

Concurrent Archimedean induction with restriction for signed-digit represented compact sets
is the following rule

\frac{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}})\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow P(K))))}{(\forall K\in\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}})\,P(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{0\,\mathrel{\not\varepsilon}\,K}}\,\,\mathrm{(CAICR)}

where
P=\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(P^{\prime})
for some non-Harrop predicate
P^{\prime}
.

Proposition 37
.

Concurrent Archimedean induction with restriction for
signed-digit represented compact sets
(CAICR)
is realisable.
More precisely, let
g
be the canonical realiser of
Rule
(
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
-
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-absorb
)
, namely
g=\mathbf{mapamb}\,\,\mathbf{Right}
,
and let
s
realise the premise of (CAICR).
Set

s^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\lambda f.\,\lambda u.\,\mathbf{case}\,u\,\mathbf{of}\,\{\mathbf{Left}(u^{\prime})\to u^{\prime};\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(u^{\prime\prime},d))\to d\mathopen{\downarrow}(f\,u^{\prime\prime})\}.

Then the conclusion of (CAICR) is realised by

f\,b\overset{rec}{=}g\mathopen{\downarrow}\mathbf{mapamb}\,(s^{\prime}f)\,(s\,b).

Proof 9.5
.

Let
b\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. We have to show

1.

0\,\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}K\to f\,b\not=\bot
,

2.

f\,b\not=\bot\to(f\,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
.

(
1
)
Since
b\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
it follows that
s\,b=\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
and

\displaystyle f\,b

\displaystyle=g\mathopen{\downarrow}\mathbf{mapamb}\,(s^{\prime}f)\,(s\,b)

\displaystyle=g\mathopen{\downarrow}\mathbf{Amb}((s^{\prime}f)\mathopen{\downarrow}u,(s^{\prime}f)\mathopen{\downarrow}v)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Amb}(\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}((s^{\prime}f)\mathopen{\downarrow}u),\mathbf{Right}\mathopen{\downarrow}((s^{\prime}f)\mathopen{\downarrow}v))\not=\bot

(
2
) Again we use Scott induction. For
i\in\mathbb{N}
let

\displaystyle f_{0}\,b\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\bot,

\displaystyle f_{i+1}\,b\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}g\mathopen{\downarrow}\mathbf{mapamb}\,(s^{\prime}f_{i})\,(s\,b).

By the continuity of function application, if
f\,b\not=\bot
then
f_{i}\,b\not=\bot
, for some
i\in\mathbf{N}
. Therefore, it suffices to show

(\forall i\in\mathbf{N})\,(b\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\wedge f_{i}\,b\not=\bot\to(f\,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)).

We induce on
i
. The induction base is trivial as
f_{0}\,b=\bot
. For the induction step assume that
b\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
and
f_{i+1}\,b\not=\bot
. As we have seen above,
s\,b=\mathbf{Amb}(u,v)
. Hence,
f_{i+1}\,b=\mathbf{Amb}((s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}u,(s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}v)
. Moreover,
u\not=\bot
or
v\not=\bot
, and for
k\in\{u,v\}
with
k\not=\bot
,
(s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}k=s^{\prime}f_{i}\,k
as well as

k\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(P(2(K_{0}))\to P(K)))).

We show that
(s^{\prime}f_{i}\,k)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
.

If
k=\mathbf{Left}(k^{\prime})
with
k^{\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
, then
s^{\prime}f_{i}\,k=k^{\prime}
. Hence we are done. In the other case
k=\mathbf{Right}(\mathbf{Pair}(k^{\prime\prime},d))
where
k^{\prime\prime}\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathbf{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
and
d\,\mathbf{r}\,(P(2(K_{0}))\to P(K))
. Then
s^{\prime}f_{i}\,k=d\mathopen{\downarrow}(f_{i}\,k^{\prime\prime})
. Since
f_{i+1}\,b\not=\bot
, we have that also
(s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}k\not=\bot
, as otherwise
s^{\prime}f_{i}=\bot
and hence
\mathbf{Amb}((s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}u,(s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}v)=\bot
as well as
g\mathopen{\downarrow}\mathbf{Amb}((s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}u,(s^{\prime}f_{i})\mathopen{\downarrow}v)=\bot
. Thus,
d\mathopen{\downarrow}(f_{i}\,k^{\prime\prime})\not=\bot
. Because application is strict, it follows that
f_{i}\,k^{\prime\prime}\not=\bot
. By the induction hypothesis we therefore have that
(f\,k^{\prime\prime})\,\mathbf{r}\,P(2(K_{0}))
. Hence,
d\mathopen{\downarrow}(f\,k^{\prime\prime})\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
. It follows that
(\mathbf{mapamb}\,(s^{\prime}f)\,(s\,b))\,\mathbf{r}\,\mathord{\downdownarrows}(P(K)
and consequently
(f\,b)\,\mathbf{r}\,P(K)
.

We extend the rules of CFP by the new Rules (AICR) and (CAICR).

In the following we will use that the elements of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})

are decidable classical subsets of
\mathbf{SD}
:

Lemma 38
.

If
E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
, then

1.

(\forall d\in\mathbf{SD})\,(d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E\lor d\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}E)

2.

(\forall d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)\,\neg\neg(d\in\mathbf{SD})

Proof 9.6
.

Easy induction on
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))
. In part (
1
),

\mathbf{SD}
could be replaced
by any discrete predicate, that is, predicate
P
such that

(\forall x,y\in P)\,(x=y\lor x\not=y)
. In part (
2
),
\mathbf{SD}
could be
replaced by any predicate.

Let

\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(K\leq 0\vee K\geq 0)\vee(K_{-1}\not=\emptyset\land K_{1}\neq\emptyset).

Proposition 39
.

If
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
, then
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{0\,\mathrel{\not\varepsilon}\,K}
.

Proof 9.7
.

It suffices to verify the premise of Rule (AICR) with
P(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. That is we must show that

\mathbf{B}_{{\mathbf{K}}}(K)\vee(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\wedge(\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K))).

Since
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
, there is some
E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
with

K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}
so that
K_{d}
is non-empty, for all
d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E
.
Thanks to Lemma
38
(
1
), we can do a case analysis on
the elements of
E
.

Case

0\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}E
, that is,
E\subseteq\mathbf{GC}
.

In this case, we have that both
K_{-1}
and
K_{1}
are not empty, if
E=\mathbf{GC}
;
K\leq 0
, if
1\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}E
; and
K\geq 0
,
if
-1\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}E
. Hence
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
holds.

Case

0\mathrel{\varepsilon}E
.

Then
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
, by the definition of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
. It remains to show that
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))\rightarrow\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. Assume that
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
. Then also
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K_{0})
.

If both
K_{0}\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{-1}
and
K_{0}\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
are not empty,
K_{-1}
and

K_{1}
are not empty as well. In case
K_{0}\leq 0
, then
K\leq 0
, if, in addition,

1\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}E
. Otherwise,
K_{-1}\not=\emptyset
and
K_{1}\not=\emptyset
;
similarly, if
K_{0}\geq 0
. Thus,
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
.

10.
Signed digit and Gray code for non-empty compact sets

In this section the Gray code representation of non-empty compact sets is introduced
and its connection with the signed digit representation of these sets is studied.

{defi}

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathbf{K}(K)\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])).

Our first goal is to show that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}
. To this end we need the following results.

Lemma 40
.

If
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
then also

1.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K)
.

2.

\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
.

3.

\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
.

4.

(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]))
.

Proof 10.1
.

(
1
) Let
P\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\mid\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K)\,\}
. We use co-induction to prove that
P\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
. That is, we show that

P(K)\rightarrow(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\wedge(\forall d\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge P(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))).

Since
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K)
, there is some
F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
so that
-K=\bigcup\{\,(-K)_{d}\mid d\in F\,\}
and for all
d\mathrel{\varepsilon}F
,
(-K)_{d}\not=\emptyset
as well as
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[(-K)_{d}])
. Note that
(-K)_{d}=-(K_{(-d)})
and
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[(-K)_{d}]=-\mathbf{av}_{-d}^{-1}[K_{(-d)}]
. Therefore, we can choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,-d\mid d\mathrel{\varepsilon}F\,\}
.

(
2
)
The proof is by co-induction.
Let
R\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}\mid(\exists K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}})\,x=\mathbf{min}\,K\,\}
.
We show

R(x)\rightarrow(\exists d\in\mathbf{SD})\,(x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}\land R(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(x))).

If
x\in R
then
x=\mathbf{min}\,K
, for some
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
.
Hence, there exists
E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
so that

K=\bigcup\{\,K_{e}\mid e\mathrel{\varepsilon}E\,\}
.
Moreover,
K_{e}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{e}])
,
for all
e\mathrel{\varepsilon}E
.
Order
\mathbf{SD}
by
-1<0<1
and let
d
be the least element of
E
with
respect to this order (which can be determined, thanks to
Lemma
38
(
1
)).
Then
\mathbf{min}\,K\in K_{d}\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}
and

\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\in\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]
. Note that
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}
is monotone.
Therefore,
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)=\mathbf{min}\,\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]
.
Since
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
, it follows that

\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\in R
.

(
3
)
The statement follows easily with the first two statements and
[
BT21a
, Lemma 23]
, stating that
\mathbf{S}
is closed under
\lambda x.\,{-}x
,

(
4
)
Let
d\in\mathbf{GC}
and set

Q^{d}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,L\mid(\exists K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}})\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge L=\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])\,\}.

We use half-strong co-induction to show that
Q^{d}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
.
That is, we prove

Q^{d}(L)\rightarrow((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(L\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\wedge(\forall e\mathrel{\varepsilon}E)\,(L_{e}\not=\emptyset\wedge Q^{d}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[L_{e}]))))\vee\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(L).

Assume that
Q^{d}(L)
. Then there is some
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
such that
K_{d}\not=\emptyset
and
L=\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]
. Since
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
,
there is some
F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
so that

•

K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{F}
and

•

(\forall f\mathrel{\varepsilon}F)\,(K_{f}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{f}^{-1}[K_{f}]))
.

We perform a case analysis on whether
d\mathrel{\varepsilon}F
using
Lemma
38
(
1
).

If
d\mathrel{\varepsilon}F
, we have that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])
. If
d=-1
, then
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(x)=2x+1=\mathbf{t}(x)
. Thus,
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]=\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]=L
. That is, we have that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(L)
.
On the other hand, if
d=1
, then
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(x)=2x-1=-\mathbf{t}(x)
. Hence,
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]=-L
. It follows that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(-L)
, whence we obtain that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(L)
.

If
d\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}F
, then
F\subseteq\{0\}\cup\{-d\}
, by Lemma
38
(
2
).

Case

0\mathrel{\varepsilon}F
.

Then
K_{d}\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{0}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(2(K_{0}))
. Furthermore,

2(K_{d})=2(K\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{0}\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d})=2(K_{0})\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}

and

\mathbf{av}_{1}^{-1}[L]=-\mathbf{t}[L]=-\mathbf{t}[\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]]=\mathbf{t}[2(K_{d})]=\mathbf{t}[2(K_{0})\cap\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}],

from which it follows that
Q^{d}(\mathbf{av}_{1}^{-1}[L])
.
Moreover,
L=\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]\subseteq\mathbf{t}[\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{0}]=\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}

and hence
L_{1}=L\not=\emptyset
.
Therefore, we have proven the left part of the disjunction with
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{1\}
.

Case

0\mathrel{\not\!\varepsilon}F
.

Now,
F=\{-d\}
. Hence
K_{d}=\{0\}
and
L=\{1\}
.
As it follows by co-induction that
\{\{-1\},\{1\}\}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
,
we have
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(L)
.

With Theorem
7
and Lemma
40
we now obtain by co-induction what we were looking for.

Proposition 41
.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}
.

Remark 42
.

Inspecting the proof of Part (4) of Lemma
40
,
one sees that the extracted realiser
yields a defined result for
every

d\in\mathrm{SD}
,
even if
K_{d}=\emptyset
. In that case the computed realiser of the implication

K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
is defined but does not
necessarily realise
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
. Therefore,
this implication cannot be strengthened to a restriction.
The computation contained in the proof of (4) takes as input only
d
and
F

(more precisely, realisers of
d\in\mathrm{SD}
and
F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
)
and a realiser of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
.
It does not use the information that
K_{d}
is non-empty.
This information is only needed to prove the correctness of the result.
Thus the transformation extracted from the proof of Proposition
41

outputs for every realiser of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
a
total
full binary tree,
that is, the addresses of nodes are
all
finite sequences of elements in
\mathbf{GC}
.
Each node
\vec{d}=d_{0},\ldots,d_{n-1}
is labelled by a pair of infinite Gray codes
such that with
g_{\vec{d}}=[g_{d_{0}},\ldots,g_{d_{n-1}}]

(using the notation of Section
2
where
g_{-1}
and
g_{1}
are the
inverses of the legs of
\mathbf{t}
),
if
g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]
is non-empty, then the label
consists of realisers of

\mathbf{G}(\min g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K])
and
\mathbf{G}(\max g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K])
.
If

g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]

is empty, the label is meaningless.
It is not possible to computationally distinguish meaningful from meaningless labels
since in general a realiser of
K
does not allow us to recognise the non-emptiness
of

g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]
.
An extreme example is
K=\{0\}
where we may only ever know that the label at
the root contains reliable information, namely realisers of
\mathbf{G}(\min K)

and
\mathbf{G}(\max K)
. The labels at all other nodes may never be known to carry
correct information. This shows, in particular, that the conjuncts

\mathbf{G}(\min K)
and
\mathbf{G}(\max K)
, in the co-inductive definition of
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
cannot
be replaced by the weaker formulas
\mathbf{D}(\min K)
and
\mathbf{D}(\max K)

which would only provide the first digits of the Gray codes of
\min K
and
\max K
.
If, however, for some node
\vec{d}
the first digit of the Gray code of
\min g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]
is defined, it will tell us whether
\min g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]\leq 0
and hence
(g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K])_{-1}\neq\emptyset
, or
\min g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]\geq 0
and thus
(g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K])_{1}\neq\emptyset
; similarly for
\max g_{\vec{d}}^{-1}[K]
.

Our next aim is to show that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
. We start with a technical lemma.

Lemma 43
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K).

Proof 10.2
.

Let
R\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\mid\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K)\,\}
. We use strong co-induction to show that
R\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}
, That is, we have to show that

R(K)\rightarrow(\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to(R(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])\vee\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])))).

Assume that
R(K)
. Then
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(-K)
and hence

\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,(-K))\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,(-K))\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,((-K)_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[(-K)_{d}])).

Note that
-(K_{d})=(-K)_{(-d)}
and hence
\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]=\mathbf{t}[-(K_{d})]=\mathbf{t}[(-K)_{(-d)}]
. Moreover,
\mathbf{min}\,(-K)=-\mathbf{max}\,K
and
\mathbf{max}\,(-K)=-\mathbf{min}\,K
. Since
\mathbf{G}
is closed under
\lambda x.-x
, by
[
Be16
, Lemma 7]
, it follows that

\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])),

as was to be shown.

Lemma 44
.

For
d\in\mathbf{GC}
,

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow K_{d}\not=\emptyset\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]).

Proof 10.3
.

The statement follows by case distinction on
d
. Assume that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
.
Then
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
.

Case

d=-1
.

This case is obvious, as for
x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{-1}
,
\mathbf{t}(x)=2x+1=\mathbf{av}_{-1}^{-1}(x)
.

Case

d=1
.

For
x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
,
\mathbf{t}(x)=1-2x=-\mathbf{av}_{1}^{-1}(x)
.
Therefore the statement follows with Lemma
43
.

Lemma 45
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
. Then

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}((\lambda x.\,1-x)[K]).

Proof 10.4
.

The statement follows by co-induction. Note to this end that
(\lambda x.\,1-x)[K]\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
as well. Moreover,
\mathbf{min}\,((\lambda x.\,1-x)[K])=1-\mathbf{max}\,K
and
\mathbf{max}\,((\lambda x.\,1-x)[K])=1-\mathbf{min}\,K
. Now assume that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. Then
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
and
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
. By
[
Be16
, Lemma 10]
we have for
x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
with
\mathbf{G}(x)
that also
\mathbf{G}(1-x)
. Thus, we obtain
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,(\lambda x.\,1-x)[K])
and
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,(\lambda x.\,1-x)[K])
. Since for
x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
,
\mathbf{t}(1-x)=2x-1=\mathbf{av}_{1}^{-1}(x)
, it follows with Lemma
44
that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[(\lambda x.\,1-x)[K]])
.

Lemma 46
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}
. Then

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(1/2K)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K).

Proof 10.5
.

The statement follows again by co-induction. Assume that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(1/2K)
. Then
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K/2)
and
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K/2)
. Since by
[
Be16
, Lemma 11]

\mathbf{G}
is closed under
\lambda x.\,2x
for
|x|\leq 1/2
, it follows that
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
and
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
.

As a further consequence of our assumption we have for
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
K_{d}\neq\emptyset
that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[1/2K_{d}])
. Because
\mathbf{t}(x/2)=1-|x|
, we obtain that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}((\lambda x.\,1-|x|)[K_{d}])
. Hence,
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(|K_{d}|)
, by Lemma
45
, and therefore
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
.

Set

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{min}}_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{min}\,K\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{min}}_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{max}\,K\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{max}}_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\neq 0.

Then

\neg\neg(B^{\mathrm{min}}_{0}\lor B^{\mathrm{min}}_{1})

and

\neg\neg(B^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\lor B^{\mathrm{max}}_{1}).

It follows for

C_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}B^{\mathrm{min}}_{i}\land B^{\mathrm{max}}_{j}

with
i,j\in\{0,1\}
that

\neg\neg(\bigvee_{0\leq i,j\leq 1}C_{i,j}).

(7)

Moreover, all
C_{i,j}
are Harrop.

Now, let

A(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\neq\emptyset).

Lemma 47
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))
.

Proof 10.6
.

Assume
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
. Because of (
7
) and the Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\lor
), (
\mathord{\restriction}
-stab
), and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mp
),
it suffices to show

1.

A(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,0}}
,

2.

A(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,1}}
,

3.

A(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1,0}}
,

4.

A(K)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1,1}}
.

The assumption
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
entails that
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
and
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
.
Hence, we have for
x\in\{\mathbf{min}\,K,\mathbf{max}\,K\}
that

\qquad(x\geq 0\lor x\leq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\neq 0}.

(8)

Since from
\mathbf{G}(x)
we obtain that also
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{t}(x))
, it follows in the same way that

\qquad(\mathbf{t}(x)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(x)\leq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{t}(x)\neq 0}.

(9)

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\leftrightarrow K\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0\leftrightarrow K_{-1}\neq\emptyset,

\displaystyle\mathbf{max}\,K\leq 0\leftrightarrow K\leq 0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 0\leftrightarrow K_{1}\neq\emptyset.

(
1
)
Observe that
C_{0,0}
is the formula
(\mathbf{min}\,K\neq 0\land\mathbf{max}\,K\neq 0)
.
We use (
8
) for
x=\mathbf{min}\,K
and
x=\mathbf{max}\,K
.
With Rules (
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\land
), (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon), and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-antimon) we then obtain

((\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{max}\,K\leq 0))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{min}\,K\neq 0\land\mathbf{max}\,K\neq 0}

which is equivalent to

(\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\lor(\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0\land\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 0)\lor\mathbf{max}\,K\leq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,0}}

and, by the above equivalences, to

(K\geq 0\lor(K_{-1}\neq\emptyset\land K_{1}\neq\emptyset)\lor K\leq 0)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,0}}.

Since the formula

(K\geq 0\lor(K_{-1}\neq\emptyset\land K_{1}\neq\emptyset)\lor K\leq 0)

clearly implies
A(K)
, we are done by Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon).

(
2
)

C_{0,1}
is the formula
(\mathbf{min}\,K\neq 0\land\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\neq 0)
.
We use (
8
) for
x=\mathbf{min}\,K
and (
9
) for
x=\mathbf{max}\,K
.
With a similar argument as in the previous case we receive

((\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\leq 0))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,1}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
A(K)
is implied by the formula

(\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\leq 0)).

The latter is equivalent to

(K\geq 0\lor K_{-1}\neq\emptyset)\land(|\mathbf{max}\,K|\leq 1/2\lor|\mathbf{max}\,K|\geq 1/2).

If
K\geq 0
, we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{1\}
.

If
K_{-1}\neq\emptyset
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\leq 1/2
we chose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{-1,0\}
.

If
K_{-1}\neq\emptyset
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\geq 1/2
we
have
\mathbf{max}\,K\neq 0
and can therefore use (
8
) and Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mp
)
to get
\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{max}\,K\leq 0
, that is,
K_{1}\neq\emptyset\lor K\leq 0
.

If
K_{1}\neq\emptyset
, we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{-1,1\}
. If
K\leq 0
, we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{-1\}
.

(
3
) is dual to (
2
).

(
4
)

C_{1,1}
is the formula
(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\neq 0\land\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\neq 0)
.
Using (
9
) for
x=\mathbf{min}\,K
and
x=\mathbf{max}\,K
we obtain

((\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\leq 0))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1,1}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
A(K)
is implied by the formula

(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\geq 0\lor\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\leq 0).

The latter is equivalent to

(|\mathbf{min}\,K|\leq 1/2\lor|\mathbf{min}\,K|\geq 1/2)\land(|\mathbf{max}\,K|\leq 1/2\lor|\mathbf{max}\,K|\geq 1/2).

If
|\mathbf{min}\,K|\leq 1/2
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\leq 1/2
, we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{0\}
.

If
|\mathbf{min}\,K|\leq 1/2
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\geq 1/2
, then
\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 1/2
, hence we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{0,1\}
.

If
|\mathbf{min}\,K|\geq 1/2
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\leq 1/2
, then
\mathbf{min}\,K\leq-1/2
, hence we choose
E\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{-1,0\}
.

If
|\mathbf{min}\,K|\geq 1/2
and
|\mathbf{max}\,K|\geq 1/2
, then, by (
9
),

(\mathbf{min}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{min}\,K\leq 0)\land(\mathbf{max}\,K\geq 0\lor\mathbf{max}\,K\leq 0)
, which, as in
case (
1
), implies
A(K)
.

The above statements now allow the derivation of the result we are looking for.

Proposition 48
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}.

Proof 10.7
.

The statement follows by co-induction. We have to show that

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\land\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

From Lemmas
44
and
46
it follows

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset)\\
\rightarrow(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

By the monotonicity of
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
we thus obtain

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset))\\
\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))))),

where, the assumption

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset))

can be discharged by Lemma
47
.

The result we have obtained so far is analogous to the number case.

Theorem 49
.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
.

Remark 50
.

The definition of
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)
(Definition
10
)
can be simplified to

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)=\mathbf{K}(K)\land\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])).

This is equivalent to
10
since
\mathbf{G}
is closed under the function

\mathbf{t}
and for
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
K_{d}\neq\emptyset
,

\mathbf{K}(K)
implies
\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
,
and if
d=-1
, then
\mathbf{min}\,\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]=\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
while
for
d=1
,
\mathbf{min}\,\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]=\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
.
Although the new definition looks more complicated, it leads to simpler
realisers since in each recursion step it refers to
\mathbf{G}
only once.
The definition of
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)
in the subsequent Section
11
can be
simplified in a similar way.

11.
Concurrent Gray code for non-empty compact sets

Next, set

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\overset{\nu}{=}\mathbf{K}(K)\land\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])).

Our next and final goal is to show that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
.

Lemma 51
.

If
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)
then also

1.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)
.

2.

\mathbf{S}^{*}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
.

3.

\mathbf{S}^{*}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
.

4.

(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}]))
.

Proof 11.1
.

(
1
) Let
P\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\mid\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)\,\}
. We use co-induction to prove that
P\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
. That is, we have to show that

P(K)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge P(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

By definition of
P
it suffices to derive

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(-K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{F}\land(\forall d\in F)\,((-K)_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[(-K)_{d}]))))\\
\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge P(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

Because of the monotonicity rule for
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
we thus only have to show that

(\exists F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(-K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{F}\land(\forall d\in F)\,((-K)_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[(-K)_{d}])))\\
\rightarrow(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge P(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))),

which has been done in the proof of Lemma
40
(
1
).

(
2
) The proof is an adaptation of the proof of Lemma
40
(
2
). Let

R\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}\mid(\exists K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*})\,x=\mathbf{min}\,K\,\}.

We have to show that

R(K)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists d\in\mathbf{SD})\,(x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}\land R(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(x)))).

Assume
R(K)
. Then there is some
K\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
with
x=\mathbf{min}\,K
. It follows that

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall e\in E)\,(K_{e}\neq\emptyset\land\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{e}])))).

Because of the monotonicity law for
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
it suffices to prove that

(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall e\in E)\,(K_{e}\neq\emptyset\land\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{e}])))\to\\
(\exists d\in\mathbf{SD})\,(x\in\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}\land R(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(x))).

Order
\mathbf{SD}
again by
-1<0<1
and let
d
be the least element of
E
with respect to this order. Then
\mathbf{min}\,K\in K_{d}\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{d}
and
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\in\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]
. Note that
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}
is monotone. Therefore,
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)=\mathbf{min}\,\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]
. Since
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
, it follows that
\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\in R
.

(
3
) As in Lemma
40
, the statement is a direct consequence of Statements
1
and
2
as well as Lemma
20
(
1
).

(
4
) Set

R^{d}_{K}(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\mid(\exists Z\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*})\,(Z_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge K=\mathbf{t}[Z_{d}])\,\}.

We use concurrent half-strong co-induction to show that
R^{d}_{K}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
. That is, we have to prove that

\begin{split}R^{d}_{K}(K)\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})&)\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land\mbox{}\\
&(\forall e\in E)\,(K_{e}\not=\emptyset\wedge R^{d}_{K}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{e}]))))\vee\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)).\end{split}

(10)

If
R^{d}_{K}(K)
, there is some
Z\in\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
so that
Z_{d}\not=\emptyset
and
K=\mathbf{t}[Z_{d}]
. Since
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(Z)
, it follows that

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(Z\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{F}\land(\forall f\in F)\,(Z_{f}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{f}^{-1}[Z_{f}])))).

Now, assume that there is some
F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})
such that
Z=\bigcup_{f\in F}Z_{f}
and for all
f\in F
,
Z_{f}\not=\emptyset
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{f}^{-1}[Z_{f}])
. As in the proof of Lemma
40
(
4
) it follows for
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
Z_{d}\not=\emptyset
and
K=\mathbf{t}[Z_{d}]
that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)
, if
d\in F
or,
0\notin F
and
d\notin F
, and
R^{d}_{K}(\mathbf{av}_{1}^{-1}[K])
, if
d\notin F
, but
0\in F
. Thus, we have that

((\exists E^{\prime}\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E^{\prime}}\land(\forall e\in E^{\prime})\,(K_{e}\not=\emptyset\wedge R^{d}_{K}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{d}]))))\vee\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K).

With the monotonicity and the idempotency of
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
and
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\vee
distribution we therefore obtain

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists F\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(Z\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{F}\land(\forall f\in F)\,(Z_{f}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{f}^{-1}[Z_{f}]))))\to\mbox{}\\
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E^{\prime}\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E^{\prime}}\land(\forall e\in E^{\prime})\,\\
(K_{e}\not=\emptyset\wedge R^{d}_{K}(\mathbf{av}_{e}^{-1}[K_{e}]))))\vee\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)),

of which (
10
) is a direct consequence.

By co-induction we now obtain the first inclusion we are looking for.

Proposition 52
.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
.

Let us now start with proving the converse inclusion. Again we need some technical results.

Lemma 53
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)
.

Proof 11.2
.

Let
P\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\{\,K\mid\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)\,\}
. We prove
P\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
by strong co-induction. That is, we must show that

P(K)\to(\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\land\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\land(\forall d\in\mathbf{GC})\,(K_{d}\neq\emptyset\to(P(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])\lor\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])))).

Assume that
P(K)
. Then
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)
and hence
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(-K)
and
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[(-K)_{d}])
, for
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
(-K)_{d}\not=\emptyset
. Note that
\mathbf{t}(x)=\mathbf{t}(-x)
and
(-K)_{d}=-(K_{(-d)})
. Thus,
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
, for
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
K_{d}\not=\emptyset
. By Lemma
22
it follows that
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{max}\,K)
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{min}\,K)
. Moreover, as
\mathbf{t}(x)=\mathbf{t}(-x)
and
(-K)_{d}=-(K_{(-d)})
,
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{t}[K_{d}])
, for all
d\in\mathbf{GC}
with
K_{d}\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 54
.

For
d\in\mathbf{GC}
,

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow K_{d}\not=\emptyset\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]).

The statement follows as in case of Lemma
44
.

Lemma 55
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{1}
. Then

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}((\lambda x.\,1-x)[K]).

The proof proceeds as in Lemma
45
by using Lemma
24
.

Lemma 56
.

Let
K\in\mathbf{K}
. Then

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(1/2K)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K).

The result follows as in Lemma
46
by applying Lemma
25
.

Lemma 57
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset)).

Proof 11.3
.

The proof follows the derivation of Lemma
47
.
Set

A(K)\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset).

Then the assertion is

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K)).

Let again

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{min}}_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{min}\,K\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{min}}_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{min}\,K)\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{max}\,K\neq 0,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{max}}_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{max}\,K)\neq 0.

and

C_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{Def}}{=}B^{\mathrm{min}}_{i}\land B^{\mathrm{max}}_{j},

for
i,j\in\{0,1\}
.
As we have seen in the proof of Lemma
47
, it suffices to show that

1.

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,0}}
,

2.

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{0,1}}
,

3.

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1,0}}
,

4.

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{1,1}}
.

Assume that
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)
and note for
x\in\{\mathbf{min}\,K,\mathbf{max}\,K\}
that
\mathbf{G}^{*}(x)
entails
\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{t}(x))
. From both we obtain that
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\neq 0}
and
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{t}(x)))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathbf{t}(x)\neq 0}
. Because of Rules (
\mathord{\restriction}
-
\land
), (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
), and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-antimon
) it follows that

(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x))\land\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(y)))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\neq 0\land y\neq 0},

from which we obtain with Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-
\land
-intro
) and (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
) that

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x)\land\mathbf{B}(y))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{x\neq 0\land y\neq 0}.

(11)

As we have seen in the proof of Lemma
47
,

(\mathbf{B}(x)\land\mathbf{B}(y))\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K)),

for each choice of
x
and
y
.
With Rules (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-mon
) and (
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
-idem
) we thus have that

\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(\mathbf{B}(x)\land\mathbf{B}(y))\to\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K)).

Consequently, by (
11
) and Rule (
\mathord{\restriction}
-mon
), we obtain that
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}(A(K))\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{C_{i,j}}
, for
i,j\in\{0,1\}
.

Proposition 58
.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}
.

Proof 11.4
.

The statement follows by co-induction. We need to show that

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathrm{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

From Lemmas
54
and
56
it follows

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(K)\rightarrow((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD})\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset)\\
\rightarrow(\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}])))).

By monotonicity of
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
we thus obtain

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}(K)\rightarrow(\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset))\\
\rightarrow\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,(K_{d}\not=\emptyset\wedge\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}(\mathbf{av}_{d}^{-1}[K_{d}]))))),

where, the assumption
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}((\exists E\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{fin}}(\mathbf{SD}))\,(K\subseteq\mathbf{I}\!\mathbf{I}_{E}\land(\forall d\in E)\,K_{d}\not=\emptyset))
can be discharged by Lemma
57
.

As a consequence of Propositions
52
and
58
we now obtain our central result for the compact sets case.

Theorem 59
.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{K}}^{*}.

12.
Conclusion

In this paper the computational power of infinite Gray code has been re-considered and compared with the signed digit representation which is mostly used in applications. Infinite Gray code is a redundancy-free representation of the real numbers, whereas the signed digit representation has a high degree of redundancy: every real number has infinitely many names. Instead of all real numbers only the interval [-1, 1] was considered.

The central aim was to study the relationship between both representations without having to discuss the manipulation of code words directly. To this end, for each of the two kinds of representation, co-inductive characterisations for the spaces under consideration were introduced in a formal logical system as predicates
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{S}
, from which the representation can be recovered via a realisability interpretation. Instead of dealing with representations directly, the predicates were compared. Computable translations between the representations can be extracted from the formal proofs. The proofs also guarantee the correctness of the extracted programs.

As was known from earlier studies by Tsuiki
[
Ts02
,
TS05
]
, infinite Gray code can be translated into signed digit code in a sequential way; for the converse translation, however, one has to allow the computations to proceed concurrently. In
[
BT21b
]
, Berger and Tsuiki introduced a modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
for concurrency.
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
has no effect on the classical validity of the formula
A
, but on its realisability interpretation: two concurrent processes try to realise
A
, in case
\mathord{\downdownarrows}(A)
is realisable, at least one of them will do so. With help of this modality a predicate
\mathbf{S}_{2}
was co-inductively defined, the realisers of which are again streams of signed digits. However, they can be computed concurrently. It was shown that
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{G}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
.

In the present paper the set of rules coming with the modality
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
was enlarged by two new realisable rules, and several other useful rules were derived.
Moreover, the modality was inductively extended to a modality
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
of bounded non-determinism, co-inductively leading to predicates
\mathbf{G}^{*}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
. Proof rules for the new modality were derived, and by this way it was shown that
\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{S}^{*}
, thus extending the result in
[
BT21b
]
.

A powerful proof tool in the proof of the inclusion
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
case was Archimedean
induction. Here, a similar rule was presented for the concurrent case.

In
[
BS16
,
Sp21
]
the present authors have given a co-inductive characterisation of the hyperspace of all non-empty compact subsets of a given digit space. Instead of streams of digits, as in the point case, extracted realisers are now finitely branching infinite trees with nodes being labelled with digits. By doing so, in particular a canonical way of lifting the signed digit representation of the real numbers in
[-1,1]
to a representation of the non-empty compact subsets of
[-1,1]
is obtained. The representation is very natural: the infinite paths of a tree representing a compact set
K
correspond to the streams representing the elements of
K
.

A central aim of the present research was to do analogous investigations for the lifted representations as was done in the point case. The situation turned out very similar to the point case. Predicates
\mathbf{G}_{K},\mathbf{S}_{K}
and
\mathbf{S}_{K}^{*}
were defined co-inductively and the inclusions
\mathbf{S}_{K}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{K}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{K}^{*}
shown. Note, however, that for the last inclusion one had to use the stronger modality
\mathord{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\mathord{\downdownarrows}}}}
in the definition of a predicate for ‘concurrent’ signed digit representation, whereas in the point case the use of
\mathord{\downdownarrows}
sufficed. A co-inductive predicate
\mathbf{G}_{K}^{*}
for ‘concurrent’ Gray code was introduced as well and
\mathbf{G}_{K}^{*}=\mathbf{S}_{K}^{*}
derived.

Moreover, an Archimedean induction rule for non-empty compact subsets was obtained.

A computability-theoretic approach to representing compact sets is carried out in work by Pauly and Tsuiki
[
PT
]
who show in particular that
\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{I})
has a faithful
\mathbb{T}^{\omega}
-representation
\mathbb{T}^{\omega}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{I})
. Here,
\mathbb{T}
is the partial order
(\{\bot,0,1\},\sqsubseteq)
with
\bot\sqsubseteq 0,1
. In this study compact sets are represented as trees as well, but then converted to bottomed sequences in such a way that for finite sets the number of bottoms in the sequence increased by 1 coincides with the cardinality of the set.
The exact relationship of this kind of Gray code for
\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{I})
with the one introduced in the present paper will have to be investigated in future work.
Since the constructions given by Pauly and Tsuiki use coding and dove-tailing techniques,
which correspond to a direct reference to a fixed operational semantics, it is unclear whether
they can be recast in our abstract setting.
